Around the Quantum Lenard-Balescu equation
Le Bihan, Corentin

Abstract
. In the mean-field regime, a gas of quantum particles with Boltzmann statistics can be described by the Hartree-Fock equation. This dynamics becomes trivial if the initial distribution of particle is invariant by translation. However, the first correction is given on time of order O ⁡ ( N ) O(N) by the quantum Lenard–Balescu equation. In the first part of the present article, we justify this equation until time of order O ⁡ ( ( log ⁡ N ) 1 − δ ) O((\log N)^{1-\delta}) (for any δ ∈ ( 0 , 1 ) \delta\in(0,1) ). A similar phenomenon exists in the classical setting (with a similar validity time obtained by Duerinckx [ Due21 ] ). In a second time, we prove the convergence for dimension d ≥ 2 d\geq 2 of the solutions of the quantum Lenard–Balescu equation to the solutions of its classical counterpart in the semi-classical limit. This problem can be interpreted as a grazing collision limit: the quantum Lenard–Balescu equation looks like a cut-off Boltzmann equation, when the classical one looks like the Landau equation.



1.
Introduction

1.1.
General presentation

Fix
L>0
. We denote
\mathbb{T}_{L}
the torus of length
L
,
(\mathbb{R}/L\mathbb{Z})^{d}
(with
d\geq 2
) and
\mathfrak{H}_{L}
the Hilbert space
L^{2}(\mathbb{T}_{L})
. We want to describe
N
particles in
\mathbb{T}_{L}
interacting
via
pairwise potential energy. In the following, we denote
\mathbb{Z}^{d}_{L}:=(2\pi L\mathbb{Z})^{d}
the dual space of
\mathbb{T}_{L}
.

We fix
\mu
,
L
and
N
such that
\mu=NL^{-d}
. The state
\psi_{N}(t)\in\mathfrak{H}_{L}^{N}
follows the Schrodinger dynamics

(1.1)

i\hbar\partial_{t}\psi_{N}(t)=\frac{\hbar^{2}}{2}\sum_{j=1}^{N}-\triangle_{j}\psi_{N}(t)+\frac{1}{\mu}\sum_{j<k}^{N}V_{j,k}\psi_{N}(t)

with
\triangle_{j}
the Laplacian with respect to the
j
-th variable and
V_{j,k}
the multiplication by
\mathscr{V}(x_{j}-x_{k})
. For the moment, one can suppose
\mathscr{V}
smooth, with spherical symmetry and compact support.

With a statistic point of view, the system is described by a density matrix
F_{N}\in\mathscr{L}^{1}(\mathfrak{H}_{L})
(the space of trace class operators, see Section 1.9 of
[
Gol13
]
for the definition and basic properties). An easy way to introduce
F_{N}
is to understand it as a Hilbert-Schmidt operator with Kernel
F_{N}(x_{[1,N]},y_{[1,N]})\in L^{2}(\mathfrak{H}_{L}^{2N})
, with
x_{[1,N]}:=(x_{1},\cdots,x_{N})\in\mathbb{T}^{N}_{L}

(1.2)

F_{N}(x_{[1,N]},y_{[1,N]}):=\int\psi(x_{[1,N]})\bar{\psi}_{N}(y_{[1,N]})\pi\left(\hskip 1.0pt\mathrm{d}\psi\right)

where
\pi
is a probability measure on
\{\psi\in L^{2}(\mathfrak{H}_{L}^{N}),\|\psi\|=1\}
.

As particles are exchangeable, the matrix
F_{N}
has to verify the following (weak) symmetry condition:

(1.3)

\forall\sigma\in\mathfrak{S}_{N},\penalty\ F_{N}(x_{\sigma(1)},\cdots,x_{\sigma(N)},y_{\sigma(1)},\cdots,y_{\sigma(N)})=F_{N}(x_{1},\cdots,x_{N},y_{1},\cdots,y_{N}).

Remark 1.1
.

We say that
F_{N}
is

•

Fermionic if
\pi
has support included in (with
\varepsilon(\sigma)
the signature of
\sigma\in\mathfrak{S}_{n}
)

\displaystyle\{\psi\in\mathfrak{H}_{L}^{N},\penalty\ \psi(x_{\sigma(1)},\cdots,x_{\sigma(N)})=\epsilon(\sigma)\psi(x_{1},\cdots,x_{N})\},

\displaystyle{\it id\penalty\ est}\penalty\ F_{N}(x_{1},\cdots,x_{N},y_{\sigma(1)},\cdots,y_{\sigma(N)})=\epsilon(\sigma)F_{N}(x_{1},\cdots,x_{N},y_{1},\cdots,y_{N}),

•

Bosonic if
\pi
has support included in

\displaystyle\{\psi\in\mathfrak{H}_{L}^{N},\penalty\ \psi(x_{\sigma(1)},\cdots,x_{\sigma(N)})=\psi(x_{1},\cdots,x_{N})\},

\displaystyle{\it id\penalty\ est}\penalty\ F_{N}(x_{1},\cdots,x_{N},y_{\sigma(1)},\cdots,y_{\sigma(N)})=F_{N}(x_{1},\cdots,x_{N},y_{1},\cdots,y_{N}).

Using (
1.1
) and (
1.2
), the density matrix
F_{\hbar,N}(t)
is solution of the Von Neumann equation

(1.4)

i\hbar\partial_{t}F_{\hbar,N}(t)=\frac{\hbar^{2}}{2}\sum_{j=1}^{N}\left[-\triangle_{j},F_{\hbar,N}(t)\right]+\frac{1}{\mu}\sum_{j<k}^{N}\left[V_{j,k},F_{\hbar,N}(t)\right],\penalty\ F_{\hbar,N}(t=0):=F_{N,0}.

At time
0
, we make a
chaos assumption
,
i.e.
we suppose the factorization of the density matrix: there exists
F_{0}\in\mathscr{L}^{1}(\mathfrak{H}_{L})
, non-negative, and with
\tr F_{0}=L^{d}
, such that the density matrix
F_{N,0}
verifies

(1.5)

F_{N,0}(x_{1},\cdots,x_{n},y_{1},\cdots,y_{n})=\prod_{i=1}^{N}L^{-d}F_{0}(x_{i},y_{i}).

We choose
\tr F_{0}=L^{d}
in order to have formally
\|F_{0}\|_{L^{\infty}}
of order
1
(it will be useful when we will take a large box limit
L\to\infty
).

Derivation of an effective equation on the long time scale

Define
F^{(n)}{\hbar,N}
the
n
-particles density matrix

(1.6)

\forall n\in[1,N],\penalty\ F_{\hbar,N}^{(n)}(t,x_{[1,n]},y_{[1,n]}):=\int F_{\hbar,N}(t,x_{[1,n]},z_{[n+1,N]},y_{[1,n]},z_{[n+1,N]})\hskip 1.0pt\mathrm{d}{z_{[n+1,N]}}

In that setting, one can prove (see
[
Gol13
,
PPS19
]
) that when
N\to\infty
with
\mu=NL^{-d}
(and
L
fixed), the one particle density matrix
F_{\hbar,N}^{(1)}(t,x_{1},y_{1})
converges to
L^{-d}F_{\hbar}(t,x,y)
, the solution of the solution of Hartree equation

(1.7)

\left\{\begin{split}i\hbar\partial_{t}F_{\hbar}(t)&=-\hbar^{2}\big[\tfrac{1}{2}\triangle,F_{\hbar}\big]+\big[\tilde{\mathscr{V}},F_{\hbar}\big],\penalty\ F_{\hbar}(t=0)=F_{0},\\
\tilde{\mathscr{V}}(t,x)&=\int\mathscr{V}(x-y)F_{\hbar}(t,y,y)\hskip 1.0pt\mathrm{d}{y}.\end{split}\right.

where
\tilde{\mathscr{V}}
is an effective potential.

In
[
PPS19
]
, the authors prove a stronger result (propagation of chaos): if at time
0
the system is distributed with respect to
F_{0,N}:=L^{-Nd}F_{0}^{\otimes N}
, then
\forall t\geq 0,j\in[1,N]
,

(1.8)

\sup_{y_{[1,j]}\in\mathbb{T}^{j}_{L}}\int\left|\left(F_{\hbar,N}^{(j)}-F_{\hbar}^{\otimes j}\right)(t,x_{[1,j]},x_{[1,j]}+y_{[1,j]})\right|\hskip 1.0pt\mathrm{d}{x_{[1,j]}}\leq Ce^{C/\hbar t}\frac{j^{2}}{\mu}

where the constant
C
does not depend on
\hbar
. Note that the norm is the
\mathscr{L}^{1}(\mathfrak{H}_{L}^{j})
-operator norm.

If we choose an initial data invariant by translation (
F_{0}(x,y)=G(x-y)
), the effective potential becomes constant. Hence, at the first order, the density is constant at the first order.

However, the error terms
F_{\hbar,N}^{(1)}(t)-F_{\hbar}(t)
,
F_{\hbar,N}^{(2)}(t)-F_{\hbar}^{\otimes 2}(t)
are of order
O(1/\mu)
. Hence, it is natural to guess if one can obtain a corrector on long time (of order
O(\mu)
).

We introduce
\Phi_{\hbar,N,L}(t,v)
the scaled in time Wigner transform of
F_{\hbar,N}^{(1)}
as

(1.9)

\forall v\in\mathbb{Z}^{d}_{L/\hbar},\penalty\ \Phi_{\hbar,N,L}(t,v):=\frac{L^{d}}{(2\pi\hbar)^{d}}\int_{\mathbb{T}_{2L}}F_{\hbar,N}^{(1)}(\mu t,\tfrac{y}{2},-\tfrac{y}{2})e^{i\frac{v}{\hbar}y}\hskip 1.0pt\mathrm{d}{y}

In limit
L,\mu\to\infty
,
L^{d}\mu=N
, one can prove the formal convergence (see Theorem
1
for a precise statement) of
\Phi_{\hbar,N,L}(\mu t,v)
to the solution of the Quantum-Lenard-Balescu equation: for
\hskip 1.0pt{\rm{}^{-}\!\!\!\!d}k:=\tfrac{1}{(2\pi)^{d}}\hskip 1.0pt{\rm{}^{-}\!\!\!\!d}k
,

(QLB)

\displaystyle\forall t\geq 0,\penalty\ \forall v_{1}\in\mathbb{R}^{d},\penalty\ \partial_{t}\Phi_{\hbar}(t,v_{1})=Q^{\hbar}_{LB}(\Phi_{\hbar}(t))(v_{1}).

(1.10)

\displaystyle Q^{\hbar}_{LB}(\Phi)(v_{1})=\frac{2}{\hbar^{2}}\int\frac{\hat{\mathscr{V}}(k)^{2}\delta_{k\cdot(v_{2}-v_{1}-\hbar k)}}{|\varepsilon_{\hbar}(\Phi,k,v_{1})|^{2}}(\Phi(v_{2}-\hbar k)\Phi(v_{1}+\hbar k)-\Phi(v_{1})\Phi(v_{2}))\hskip 1.0pt{\rm{}^{-}\!\!\!\!d}k\hskip 1.0pt\mathrm{d}{v_{2}}

(1.11)

\displaystyle\varepsilon_{\hbar}(\Phi,k,v)=1+{\hat{\mathscr{V}}(k)}\int\frac{\Phi(v_{*})-\Phi(v_{*}-\hbar k)}{\hbar k\cdot(v_{*}-v-\hbar k)-i0}\hskip 1.0pt\mathrm{d}{v_{*}}.

We precise that we take the convention for the Fourier transform

(1.12)

\hat{\mathscr{V}}(k)=\int\mathscr{V}(x)e^{-ikx}\hskip 1.0pt\mathrm{d}x.

This equation looks like the Boltzmann equation, with a dynamical screening
|\varepsilon_{\hbar}(\Phi,k,v_{1})|^{-2}
, and verifies formally the same conservation laws and H-theorem

\frac{\hskip 1.0pt\mathrm{d}}{\hskip 1.0pt\mathrm{d}t}\int\Phi_{\hbar}(t,v_{1})\left(\begin{matrix}1\\
v_{1}\\
|v_{1}|^{2}\end{matrix}\right)\hskip 1.0pt\mathrm{d}{v_{1}}=0,\penalty\ \frac{\hskip 1.0pt\mathrm{d}}{\hskip 1.0pt\mathrm{d}t}\int\Phi_{\hbar}(t,v_{1})\log\Phi_{\hbar}(t,v_{1})\hskip 1.0pt\mathrm{d}{v_{1}}\leq 0.

Hence, it describes an quantitative irreversibility coming from the mixing of the system.

This equation has been first formally derived by Balescu
[
Bal61
]
for particles in the Bose-Einstein or Fermi-Dirac statistic (for these particles, he found a cubic version of the equation).

Remark 1.2
.

Let
F_{N}
be a Bosonic density matrix.

•

if there exists
F_{1}\in\mathscr{L}^{1}(\mathfrak{H}_{L})
such that
F_{N}=F_{1}^{\otimes N}
, then there exists
\psi\in\mathfrak{H}
such that
F_{1}(x,y)=\psi(x)\bar{\psi}(y)
,

•

if
F_{1}
is translation invariant, then there exists a family
(c_{k})_{k}
such that

F_{1}(x,y)=\sum_{k\in\mathbb{Z}_{L}}c_{k}e^{ik(x-y)}.

We deduce that if
F_{N}
is symmetric and invariant by translation, then there exists
k\in\mathbb{Z}_{L}^{d}
such that

F_{\hbar,N}(x_{[1,N]},y_{[1,N]})=\frac{1}{L^{Nd}}e^{ik\cdot\sum_{j=1}^{N}(x_{j}-y_{j})},

which is a fix point of the mean-field dynamic. This shows that our results cannot be applied to Bosons.

Note that the density
F_{1}^{\otimes N}
is not fermionic, hence our result cannot be applied to Fermions.

Note that in the weak coupling scaling, a quantum system can also be described by a
collisional kinetic
equation. In that setting, the interaction are given by the short range potential: the density matrix respect the equation

(1.13)

i\hbar\partial_{t}F_{N}=-{\hbar^{2}}\sum_{i=1}^{N}\left[\tfrac{1}{2}\triangle_{i},F_{N}\right]+\sqrt{\hbar}\sum_{i<j}^{N}\left[\mathscr{V}\left(\tfrac{x_{i}-x_{j}}{\hbar}\right),F_{N}\right].

with the scaling
N\hbar^{3}=1
(in dimension
3
) and
L=1
. The equation has been first formally derived by Nordheim
[
Nor28
]
, and Uehling and Uhlenbeck
[
UU33
]
(see also
[
Pul19
]
).

The classical counterpart

A similar phenomenon exists in the classical setting.

Consider a gas of
N
particles inside the domain
\mathbb{T}_{L}
, with density
\mu=N/L^{d}\to\infty
. Denoting their coordinates
(x_{1},\cdots,x_{N},v_{1},\cdots,

v_{N})
, particles follow the classical Hamiltonian dynamic linked to the energy

(1.14)

\mathscr{H}_{N}:=\sum_{i=1}^{N}\frac{|v_{i}|^{2}}{2}+\frac{1}{\mu}\sum_{i<j}^{N}\mathscr{V}(x_{i}-x_{j}).

We denote
F_{N}(t,x_{[1,n]},v_{[1,n]})
the distribution of particle. It is supposed initially factorized:

\tilde{F}_{N}(t=0,x_{[1,n]},v_{[1,n]}):=\tilde{F}_{0}(x_{1},v_{1})\cdots\tilde{F}_{0}(x_{N},v_{N})

for some probability density
F_{0}
on
\mathbb{T}_{L}\times\mathbb{R}^{d}
. Then one can show that the first marginal

\tilde{F}_{N}^{(1)}(t,x_{1},v_{1}):=\int\tilde{F}_{N}(t,x_{[1,n]},v_{[1,n]})\hskip 1.0pt\mathrm{d}{x_{[2,n]}}\hskip 1.0pt\mathrm{d}{v_{[2,n]}}

converges when
N
goes infinity to
\tilde{F}(t,x,v))
, the solution of the Vlasov equation (see Braun et Hepp
[
BH77
]
, Dobrushin
[
Dob79
]
, and a review of Golse
[
Gol25
]
)

(1.15)

\left\{\begin{split}\partial_{t}\tilde{F}(t)&=-v\cdot\nabla_{x}\tilde{F}(t)+\nabla_{x}\tilde{\mathscr{V}}\cdot\nabla_{v}\tilde{F}(t),\penalty\ \tilde{F}(t=0)=\tilde{F}_{0}\\
\tilde{\mathscr{V}}(t,x)&=\int\mathscr{V}(x-y)\tilde{F}(t,y,v)\hskip 1.0pt\mathrm{d}{y}\hskip 1.0pt\mathrm{d}{v}.\end{split}\right.,

If we suppose that the initial density is invariant by translation,
F_{0}(x,v):=L^{-d}\Phi_{0}(v)
, then the effective potential
\tilde{\mathscr{V}}
becomes constant, and the Vlasov dynamics is trivial. As in the quantum case, one shows that the scaled density

\Phi_{N}(t,v_{1}):=\int\tilde{F}_{N}(\mu t,x_{[1,n]},v_{[1,n]})\hskip 1.0pt\mathrm{d}{x_{[1,n]}}\hskip 1.0pt\mathrm{d}{v_{[2,n]}}

converges as
N\to\infty
,
NL^{d}=1
to
\Phi(t,v_{1})
, solution of the (classical) Lenard-Balescu equation

(CLB)

\displaystyle\partial_{t}\Phi=Q_{LB}^{0}(\Phi)(v_{1})

(1.16)

\displaystyle Q_{LB}^{0}(\Phi)(v_{1}):=\nabla_{v_{1}}\cdot\int B(\Phi,v_{1},v_{2})\left(\Phi(v_{2})\nabla\Phi(v_{1})-\Phi(v_{1})\nabla\Phi(v_{2})\right)\hskip 1.0pt\mathrm{d}{v_{2}}

(1.17)

\displaystyle B(\Phi,v_{1},v_{2}):=\int\frac{|{\hat{\mathscr{V}}}(k)|^{2}k\otimes k}{|\varepsilon_{0}(\Phi,k,v)|^{2}}\delta_{k\cdot(v_{1}-v_{2})}\hskip 1.0pt{\rm{}^{-}\!\!\!\!d}{k}

(1.18)

\displaystyle\varepsilon_{0}(\Phi,k,v):=1+{\hat{\mathscr{V}}}(k)\int\frac{k\cdot\nabla\Phi(v_{*})}{k\cdot(v-v_{*})-i0}\hskip 1.0pt\mathrm{d}{v_{*}}.

The equation has been derived simultaneously in the 60s by Guernsey
[
Gue61
,
Gue62
]
, Lenard
[
Len60
]
and Balescu
[
Bal60
]
. In the former two derivations, the authors used a truncation of the BBGKY hierarchy. This strategy has been by adapted by Duerinckx
[
Due21
]
and Duerinckx–Saint-Raymond
[
DSR21
]
in order to obtain a consistency result (for
L=1
fixed). Note that in the first result, the author reach the time
O((\log N)^{1-\delta})
(compare to
O(N)
of the kinetic time), when in
[
DSR21
]
, the authors reaches the longer time
O(N^{\delta})
in a linear setting (where
\delta\in(0,1)
is small enough).

The classical Lenard-Balescu equation has been studied by Duerinckx and Winter in
[
DW23
]
.

Semi-classical limit of the quantum Lenard–Balescu equation

In the semi-classical limit
\hbar\to 0
, one can proof that the quantum system "converge" to the classical one. More precisely, consider
F_{\hbar}(t,x,y)
solution of the Hartree equation with initial data
F_{0,\hbar}(x,y)
. At time
0
, we suppose that the Wigner transform of
F_{0,\hbar}
defined by

(1.19)

\tilde{F}_{\hbar,0}(x,v):=\frac{1}{(2\pi\hbar)^{d}}\int F_{0,\hbar}(x+\tfrac{y}{2},x-\tfrac{y}{2})e^{i\frac{v}{\hbar}y}\hskip 1.0pt\mathrm{d}{y}

converges to some distribution
\tilde{F}_{0}(x,v)
. Then, the Wigner transform of
F_{\hbar}(t,x,y)
converge to
\tilde{F}(t,x,v)
, the solution of the Vlasov equation with initial data
F_{0}(x,v)
.

One can ask a similar question for the quantum Lenard–Balescu equation: for
\Phi_{\hbar}(t,v_{1})
the solutions of the quantum Lenard–Balescu (
QLB
) with initial data
\Phi_{0}(v_{1})
equation converge to
\Phi(t,v_{1})
, the solution of the classical Lenard–Balescu equation (
CLB
) with the same initial data.

This problem looks like a grazing collision limit: we want to link a Boltzmann like operator

Q^{\hbar}(\Phi)(v_{1})=\int_{\mathbb{S}^{d-1}\times\mathbb{R}^{d}}\left(\Phi\left(\tfrac{v_{1}+v_{2}}{2}+\tfrac{|v_{1}-v_{2}|}{2}\sigma\right)\Phi\left(\tfrac{v_{1}+v_{2}}{2}-\tfrac{|v_{1}-v_{2}|}{2}\sigma\right)-\Phi(v_{1})\Phi(v_{2})\right)B_{\hbar}(v_{1},v_{2},\sigma)\hskip 1.0pt\mathrm{d}{\sigma}\hskip 1.0pt\mathrm{d}{v_{2}}

toward a Landau type operator

Q^{0}(\Phi)(v_{1})=\nabla_{1}\cdot\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\left(\nabla\Phi\left(v_{1}\right)\Phi\left(v_{2}\right)-\Phi(v_{1})\nabla\Phi(v_{2})\right)B_{0}(v_{1},v_{2})\hskip 1.0pt\mathrm{d}{v_{2}}.

if the collision kernel
B_{\hbar}(\Phi,\tfrac{v_{1}+v_{2}}{2},\tfrac{v_{1}-v_{2}}{2},\sigma)
concentrates on the
\sigma
with
\sigma\cdot\frac{v_{1}-v_{2}}{|v_{1}-v_{2}|}\simeq 1
. Here, we use the variable
\sigma:=\frac{2\hbar k+v_{1}-v_{2}}{|v_{1}-v_{2}|}
, which is common in the literature.

The rigorous analysis of the grazing collision limit goes back to the classical result of Arsenev and Buryak in
[
AB91
]
, and a general setting as been treated by Alexandre and Villani in
[
AV04
]
. However, the solutions consider by these two authors are very weak (they are called the
renormalized solution
). As the Lenard-Balescu kernel
B_{\hbar}(\Phi_{\hbar}(t),v_{1},v_{2},\sigma)
depends on the solution
\Phi_{\hbar}(t)
, one needs a strong notion of solution.

In
[
He14
]
, the author proved the strong convergence of the solution of the Boltzmann equation toward the solution of the Landau equation, in the case where the kernel
B_{\hbar}(\Phi_{\hbar}(t),v_{1},v_{2},\sigma)
is independent of
\Phi_{\hbar}(t)
and
\frac{v_{1}+v_{2}}{2}
, and that is not integrable with respect to the variable
\sigma
(these kernels are said
uncutoff
). In that case, the Boltzmann operator acts like a fractional Laplacian, bringing some additional parabolic regularity.

Note also the work of He, Lu, Pulvirenti and Ma around the classical limit of the quantum Boltzmann equation
[
HLP21
,
HLPZ24
]
.

In the case of Lenard–Balescu kernel, there is
cutoff
: for
{\hat{\mathscr{V}}}\in L^{1}\cap L^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{3})
, the integral

\int{\hat{\mathscr{V}}}(k)^{2}\delta_{k\cdot(v_{2}-v_{1}-\hbar k)}\hskip 1.0pt{\rm{}^{-}\!\!\!\!d}{k}
is always finite.

Notation
.

As the constants in the inequalities change along the computation, we denote
A\lesssim B
if there exists a constant
C>0
independent of
\hbar
such that
A\leq CB
. If
A\lesssim B
and
B\lesssim A
, we denote
A\simeq B
. We denote
A=O(B)
if
A\lesssim B
.

For
v_{1},v_{2},k\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
,
\hbar>0
and
g,h:\mathbb{R}^{d}\to\mathbb{C}
, we denote

\displaystyle v_{1}^{\prime}:=v_{1}+\hbar k,\penalty\ v_{2}^{\prime}:=v_{2}-\hbar k,\penalty\ v_{1}^{\prime\prime}:=v_{1}-\hbar k,\penalty\ v_{2}^{\prime\prime}:=v_{2}+\hbar k

\displaystyle\Delta\hskip-6.0pt\Delta gh_{*}:=g(v_{1})h(v_{2})+g(v_{2})h(v_{1})-g(v^{\prime}_{1})h(v^{\prime}_{2})-g(v_{2}^{\prime})h(v_{1}^{\prime})

\displaystyle\forall i\in\{1,2\},\penalty\ j\in\{\penalty\ ,^{\prime}\},\penalty\ g_{i}^{j}:=g(v_{i}^{j}),

\displaystyle\hat{k}:=\tfrac{k}{|k|},\penalty\ \hskip 1.0pt\mathrm{d}V:=\hskip 1.0pt\mathrm{d}v_{1}\hskip 1.0pt\mathrm{d}v_{2}\hskip 1.0pt{\rm{}^{-}\!\!\!\!d}k.

For
\alpha=(\alpha_{1},\cdots,\alpha_{d})\in\mathbb{N}^{d}
,
(\beta^{i})_{i\leq d}=(\beta^{i}_{j})_{i,j\leq d}\in\mathbb{N}^{d^{2}}
with
\beta^{1}+\cdots+\beta^{d}=\alpha
, and
{\mathbf{e}}_{i}:=(0,\cdots,0,\underset{i}{1},0,\cdots,0)
, we denotes

\displaystyle\binom{\alpha}{\beta^{1}\cdots\beta^{d}}=\frac{\prod_{i}\alpha_{i}!}{\prod_{i,j}\beta_{i}^{j}!},\penalty\ |\alpha|=\sum_{i}\alpha_{i}

\displaystyle\partial_{v}^{\alpha}g(v)=({\mathbf{e}}_{1}\cdot\nabla_{v})^{\alpha_{1}}\cdots({\mathbf{e}}_{d}\cdot\nabla_{v})^{\alpha_{d}}f(v).

1.2.
Statement of the results

1.2.1.
Short time validity of the quantum Lenard–Balescu equation

We begin by justifying the quantum Lenard–Balescu equation. The main goal would be reaching the kinetic time
O(N)
, but this is for the moment out of reach. However, we are able to propose a partial result, up to some time
O((\log N)^{(1-\delta)})
for any
\delta\in(0,1)
.

We consider an interaction potential
\mathscr{V}
satisfying the following assumption:

Assumption 1
.

The interaction potential
\mathscr{V}
is smooth, radial and with compact support.

Theorem 1
.

Consider a density
\Phi_{0}
such that

(1.20)

\Phi_{0}\geq 0,\penalty\ \int\Phi_{0}(v)\hskip 1.0pt\mathrm{d}{v}=1\penalty\ {\rm and}\penalty\ \|\langle v\rangle^{2d}\langle\nabla\rangle^{2}\Phi_{0}\|_{L^{\infty}}\|{\hat{\mathscr{V}}}\|_{\L^{\infty}}<\frac{1}{2}.

At
t=0
we set the system such that

(1.21)

\displaystyle F_{0,L}(x,y):=\frac{(2\pi\hbar)^{d}}{L^{2d}\mathscr{Z}_{L}}\sum_{k\in\mathbb{Z}_{L}}\Phi_{0}(\hbar k)e^{ik\cdot(x-y)},

(1.22)

\displaystyle\hat{F}_{N,0}=F_{0,L}^{\otimes N.}

where
\mathscr{Z}_{L}
is a normalization constant, and we construct
F_{N}(t):\mathbb{R}^{+}\to\mathscr{L}^{1}(\mathfrak{H}^{N}_{L})
the solution of the Von Neumann equation (
1.4
) with initial data
F_{N,0}
.

We fix the scaling
\mu,L\to\infty
with
\mu=NL^{-d}
. Then, for any diverging sequence
\tau_{N}
such that for some
\delta>0
,
\tfrac{\tau_{N}}{\hbar\mu}\ll(\log N)^{\delta}
and
L\geq 2\hbar\log\tfrac{\tau_{N}}{L}
, and for any test function
\psi\in\mathscr{C}^{\infty}_{c}(\mathbb{R}_{t}^{+}\times\mathbb{R}_{v}^{d})
, the scaled Wigner transform
\Phi_{N,L}
verifies

(1.23)

\int_{0}^{\infty}\frac{(2\pi\hbar)^{d}}{L^{d}}\sum_{v\in\mathbb{Z}_{L/\hbar}}\psi\left(t,v\right)\partial_{t}\Phi_{N,L}(\tfrac{\tau_{N}}{\mu}t,v)\hskip 1.0pt\mathrm{d}{t}\underset{N\to\infty}{\longrightarrow}\int_{0}^{\infty}\int\psi(t,v)Q^{\hbar}_{LB}(\Phi_{0})(v)\hskip 1.0pt\mathrm{d}{v}\hskip 1.0pt\mathrm{d}{t}.

Remark 1.3
.

One can choose the scaling
\mu L^{d}=N
, with
\tau_{n}\leq(\log N)^{\frac{1}{2}-\beta}
for
\beta>0
and
L\simeq\log\log N
.

The proof is given in Section
2
.

Ideas of the proof

We follow mostly the strategy of
[
Due21
]
in the classical setting. It is based on the estimation of the "defect of factorization" (also called the
cumulant
, introduce in (
2.1
)) of the marginal of the system. One can prove that for short time (of order
O((\log N)^{1-\delta})
), the correction implying three or more particles can be neglected. The remaining system of two equations gives the Lenard–Balescu evolution.

In
[
Due21
]
, the estimations of the cumulants are based on the Glauber calculus. In the present paper, we use an improvement of the cumulant estimates of
[
PPS19
]
.

Note that in general, obtaining a justification of a collisional kinetic equation from a particle system is a difficult problem. The only full (non-linear, until a kinetic time) results hold in the low density setting, for the hard spheres system
[
Lan75
,
Kin75
,
IP89
,
Spo91
,
GSRT13
,
PSS14
,
DHM24
]
, and for the interacting wave system
[
DH21
,
DH23
]
. The other results are either in a
linear setting
(when we follow a particle in a bath at equilibrium
[
vBLLS80
,
DP99
,
DR01
,
BGSR16
,
Ayi17
,
Cat18
,
MS19
,
DLB25
,
BB26
]
) or in the linearized setting (the study of the fluctuations of the empirical measure around the equilibrium
[
Spo81
,
Spo83
,
BGSR17
,
BGSRS23
,
BGSRS24
,
LB22
]
), or are consistency result (result at time 0)
[
BPS13
,
Win21
,
VW18
,
Due21
,
DSR21
]
.

1.2.2.
Property of quantum Lenard–Balescu equation around the equilibrium

We study now the property of quantum Lenard–Balescu equation. We note that the Maxwellian

M(v):=\tfrac{1}{(2\pi)^{d/2}}\exp(-\tfrac{|v|^{2}}{2})

is a steady state of the equation. We want to look at a solution close to this equilibrium:

(1.24)

F(t,v_{1})=M(v_{1})+\sqrt{M(v_{1})}g(t,v_{1}).

The equation solved by
g(t,v_{1})
is

(QLB
ℏ
-bis)

\displaystyle\partial_{t}g(t,v_{1})

\displaystyle+\mathscr{L}_{\hbar}g(t,v_{1})=\mathscr{Q}_{\hbar}(g(t),g(t))(v_{1})

where we denote
F_{h}:=M+\sqrt{M}h
and

(1.25)

\displaystyle\mathscr{L}_{\hbar}g(v_{1})

\displaystyle:=\frac{2}{\hbar^{2}}\int\Delta\hskip-6.0pt\Delta\left(g\sqrt{M_{*}}\right)\frac{\hat{\mathscr{V}}(k)^{2}\delta_{k\cdot(v_{2}-v_{1}-\hbar k)}}{|\varepsilon_{\hbar}(M,k,v_{1})|^{2}}\sqrt{M_{2}}\hskip 1.0pt{\rm{}^{-}\!\!\!\!d}k\hskip 1.0pt\mathrm{d}{v_{2}}

(1.26)

\displaystyle\mathscr{Q}_{\hbar}(g,h)(v_{1}):=

\displaystyle-\frac{2}{\hbar^{2}}\int\Delta\hskip-6.0pt\Delta(gh_{*})\frac{\hat{\mathscr{V}}(k)^{2}\delta_{k\cdot(v_{2}-v_{1}-\hbar k)}}{|\varepsilon_{\hbar}(F_{h},k,v_{1})|^{2}}\sqrt{M_{2}}\hskip 1.0pt{\rm{}^{-}\!\!\!\!d}{k}\hskip 1.0pt\mathrm{d}{v_{2}}

\displaystyle+\frac{2}{\hbar^{2}}\int\Delta\hskip-6.0pt\Delta\left(g\sqrt{M_{*}}\right)\left(\frac{\hat{\mathscr{V}}(k)^{2}\delta_{k\cdot(v_{2}-v_{1}-\hbar k)}}{|\varepsilon_{\hbar}(M,k,v_{1})|^{2}}-\frac{\hat{\mathscr{V}}(k)^{2}\delta_{k\cdot(v_{2}-v_{1}-\hbar k)}}{|\varepsilon_{\hbar}(F_{h},k,v_{1})|^{2}}\right)\sqrt{M_{2}}\hskip 1.0pt{\rm{}^{-}\!\!\!\!d}k\hskip 1.0pt\mathrm{d}{v_{2}},

where
\mathscr{L}_{\hbar}
is the Linearized Boltzmann operator.

It is well known that the operator
\mathscr{L}_{\hbar}
is a self-adjoint non-negative operator in
L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
.

One of the goals of the present paper is to describe the limit as
\hbar
goes to zero. We want to link the quantum Lenard-Balescu with its classical counterpart

For perturbative solution
F(t):=M+\sqrt{M}g(t)
, the classical equation (
CLB
) becomes

(CLB)

\displaystyle\partial_{t}g(t,v_{1})

\displaystyle+\mathscr{L}_{0}g(t,v_{1})=\mathscr{Q}_{0}(g(t),g(t))(v_{1})

where we denote
H:=M+\sqrt{M}h
and

(1.27)

\displaystyle\mathscr{L}_{0}g(v_{1})

\displaystyle:=\left(\nabla-\frac{v_{1}}{2}\right)\cdot\int B(M,v_{1},v_{2})\left[\sqrt{M_{2}}\left(\nabla_{1}+\frac{v_{1}}{2}\right)g_{1}-\sqrt{M_{1}}\left(\nabla_{2}+\frac{v_{2}}{2}\right)g_{2}\right]\sqrt{M_{2}}\hskip 1.0pt\mathrm{d}{v_{2}}

(1.28)

\displaystyle\mathscr{Q}_{0}(g,h)

\displaystyle(v_{1}):=-\left(\nabla-\frac{v_{1}}{2}\right)\cdot\int B(H,v_{1},v_{2})\left[h_{2}\nabla g_{1}+g_{2}\nabla h_{1}-h_{1}\nabla g_{2}-g_{1}\nabla h_{2}\right]\sqrt{M_{2}}\hskip 1.0pt\mathrm{d}{v_{2}}

\displaystyle+\left(\nabla-\frac{v_{1}}{2}\right)\cdot\int

\displaystyle\left(B(H,v_{1},v_{2})-B(M,v_{1},v_{2})\right)\left[\sqrt{M_{2}}\left(\nabla_{1}+\frac{v_{1}}{2}\right)g_{1}-\sqrt{M_{1}}\left(\nabla_{2}+\frac{v_{2}}{2}\right)g_{2}\right]\sqrt{M_{2}}\hskip 1.0pt\mathrm{d}{v_{2}},

with the collision kernel
B(H,v_{1},v_{2})
defined in (
1.17
).

In the following, we choose a potential
\mathscr{V}
satisfying the following assumption:

Assumption 2
.

We set the dimension
d\geq 2
. We define
\mathscr{V}
a symmetric (
\mathscr{V}(x)=\mathscr{V}(|x|)
) such that there exists two constants
C>0
and
\eta>0

(1.29)

\displaystyle|\hat{\mathscr{V}}(k)|^{2}+|\hat{\mathscr{V}}^{\prime}(k)|^{2}\leq\frac{C}{(1+|k|)^{d+3+\eta}}.

We introduce the weighted Sobolev space
\mathscr{H}^{r}\subset L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
, with norm

(1.30)

\|g\|_{r}^{2}:=\sum_{r^{\prime}=0}^{r}\left\|\left(\nabla-\frac{v}{2}\right)^{r^{\prime}}g\right\|^{2}<\infty,

where we denote
\|\penalty\ \|
the
L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
. Note that the norm
\|g\|_{r}
is equivalent to
\|(\nabla-\frac{v}{2})^{r}g\|
.

Theorem 2
.

For any
d\geq 2
and

r\geq 5

•

Cauchy problem.
There exists
\hbar_{0}>0
and
\delta>0
such that for any initial data
g_{0}\in\mathscr{H}^{r}
with
\|g_{0}\|_{r}\leq\delta
and any
\hbar<\hbar_{0}
, there exists a unique
g_{\hbar}\in\mathscr{C}^{0}_{b}(\mathbb{R}^{+},\mathscr{H}^{r})
solution of the (
QLBℏ-bis
)-equations in the mild form:

(1.31)

g_{\hbar}(t)=e^{-t{\mathscr{L}}_{\hbar}}g_{0}+\int_{0}^{t}e^{-(t-s){\mathscr{L}}_{\hbar}}\mathscr{Q}_{\hbar}(g_{\hbar}(s),g_{\hbar}(s))\hskip 1.0pt\mathrm{d}s.

In addition, one has the following bound:

(1.32)

\sup_{t\geq 0}\|g_{\hbar}(t)\|_{r}\leq\|g_{0}\|_{r}.

•

Semi-classical limit.
The sequence of
(g_{\hbar})_{\hbar<\hbar_{0}}
of solution of the (
QLBℏ-bis
)-equations converges weakly in
\mathscr{C}_{b}^{0}(\mathbb{R}^{+},\mathscr{H}^{r})
as
\hbar\to 0
to
g_{\infty}
, mild-solution of the (
CLB
)-equation with initial data
g_{\infty}(t=0)=g_{0}
:

(1.33)

g_{\infty}(t)=e^{-t{\mathscr{L}}_{0}}g_{0}+\int_{0}^{t}e^{-(t-s){\mathscr{L}}_{0}}\mathscr{Q}_{0}(g_{\infty}(s),g_{\infty}(s))\hskip 1.0pt\mathrm{d}s.

In addition, there exists some positive constant
C>0
independent of
g_{0}
and
\hbar
such that:

(1.34)

\forall t\geq 0,\penalty\ \|g_{\hbar}(t)-g_{\infty}(t)\|\leq C\hbar t\|g_{0}\|_{r}.

The proof is given in Section
4
.

Remark 1.4
.

The preceding estimates can be improve to get a similar bound on
\|g_{\hbar}(t)-g_{\infty}(t)\|_{r}
. As the computations become technical, we prefer to skip them.

Ideas of the proof

As we want to perform a semi-classical limit, we want to obtain a Cauchy theory closed to the one obtain by Duerinckx and Winter
[
DW23
]
(following the strategy of
[
Guo10
]
).

In classical case, Duerinckx and Winter construct a weighted Sobolev norm
\|\cdot\|_{0}
,

\|g\|_{0}^{2}\simeq\int\frac{|p^{\perp}_{v}\nabla g(v)|^{2}+|g(v)|^{2}}{1+|v|}+\frac{|(\text{Id}-p^{\perp}_{v})\nabla g(v)|^{2}}{1+|v|^{3}}\hskip 1.0pt\mathrm{d}v,\penalty\ p^{\perp}_{v}:=\text{Id}-\tfrac{v\otimes v}{|v|^{2}},

controlling the non-linear operator. In order to use the dissipation coming from the linear operator
\mathscr{L}_{0}
, the norm
\|\cdot\|_{0}
is bonded from below by
\mathscr{L}_{0}
: denoting
\pi_{0}
the
L^{2}
-orthogonal projector onto the space
<\sqrt{M},v_{1}\sqrt{M},\cdots v_{d}\sqrt{M},|v|^{2}\sqrt{M}>^{\perp}
,

\forall g\in\mathscr{C}^{\infty}_{c}(\mathbb{R}^{d}),\penalty\ \int g\mathscr{L}_{0}g\geq\tfrac{1}{C}\|(1-\pi_{0})g\|_{0}^{2}

for some constant
C>0
.

In the quantum case, we construct a family of norm
\|\penalty\ \|_{\hbar}

\|g\|_{\hbar}^{2}\simeq\int\frac{|g(v)|^{2}}{1+|v|}\hskip 1.0pt\mathrm{d}v+\int\frac{{\hat{\mathscr{V}}}(k)^{2}}{|k|}(g(v+\hbar k)-g(v))^{2}e^{-\alpha\frac{|v\cdot k|^{2}}{|k|^{2}}}\hskip 1.0pt\mathrm{d}v\hskip 1.0pt{\rm{}^{-}\!\!\!\!d}k

for some fix constant
\alpha>0
. In the semi-classical limit,
\|\penalty\ \|_{\hbar}
converge to
\|\penalty\ \|_{0}
(for
g
smooth enough,
\|g\|_{\hbar}\to\|g\|_{0}
), but we always have
\|f\|_{\hbar}\leq\tfrac{C}{\hbar}\|f\|
(for some constant
C>0
).

The first point of the proof is that
\|\penalty\ \|_{\hbar}
does not depend on the constant
\alpha
(see Corollary
4.2
). This is crucial in the proof that these norms control the non-linear operators
\mathscr{Q}_{\hbar}
.

The second point is that, as in the classical setting, there exists a constant
C_{*}>0
independent of
\hbar
such that

\forall g\in\mathscr{C}^{\infty}_{c}(\mathbb{R}^{d}),\penalty\ \int g\mathscr{L}_{\hbar}g\geq\tfrac{1}{C_{*}}\|(1-\pi_{0})g\|_{\hbar}^{2},

(see Proposition
4.6
). Thus, the dissipation due to the linearised operator is sufficient to control the non-linear operator, and we can close some energy estimates.

Remark 1.5
.

The construction of the constant
C_{*}
is based on a compactness argument and is not constructive.

1.2.3.
Property of quantum Lenard–Balescu equation away from the equilibrium

We study now the property of quantum Lenard–Balescu equation far from equilibrium.
In the following, we choose a potential
\mathscr{V}
satisfying the following assumption:

Assumption 3
.

We set the dimension
d\geq 2
. We define
\mathscr{V}
a symmetric (
\mathscr{V}(x)=\mathscr{V}(|x|)
) such that there exists two constants
C>0
and
\eta>0

(1.35)

\displaystyle|\hat{\mathscr{V}}(k)|^{2}+|\hat{\mathscr{V}}^{\prime}(k)|^{2}\leq\frac{C}{(1+|k|)^{2d+3+\eta}}.

Theorem 3
.

For any
d\geq 2
and

N\geq 4
,
m\geq\tfrac{d}{2}+4
, fix an initial data
\Phi_{0}\geq 0
such that
\|\langle v\rangle^{m}\Phi_{0}\|_{H^{N+1}}
and
\sup_{k,v_{1}}\tfrac{1}{|\varepsilon_{0}(\Phi_{0},k,v_{1})|^{2}}
are bounded.

•

Cauchy theory.
There exists
\hbar_{0}>0
and
T>0
such that for any
\hbar<\hbar_{0}
there exists a unique
\varphi_{\hbar}\in\mathscr{C}^{0}([0,T],H^{N}(\langle v\rangle^{2m}\hskip 1.0pt\mathrm{d}v))
solution of the (
QLB
)-equations in mild-form:

(1.36)

\varphi_{\hbar}(t)=\Phi_{0}+\int_{0}^{t}{Q}_{LB}^{\hbar}(\varphi_{\hbar}(s),\varphi_{\hbar}(s))\hskip 1.0pt\mathrm{d}s.

In addition, there exists a constant
C
depending only on
\Phi_{0}
such that

(1.37)

\sup_{t\in[0,T]}\|\langle v\rangle^{m}g_{\hbar}(t)\|_{H^{N}}\leq C.

•

Semi-classical limit.
The sequence of
(g_{\hbar})_{\hbar<\hbar_{0}}
of solution of the (
QLB
)-equations converges weakly in
\mathscr{C}_{b}^{0}([0,T],H^{N}(\langle v\rangle^{2m}\hskip 1.0pt\mathrm{d}v))
as
\hbar\to 0
to
g_{\infty}
, mild-solution of the (
CLB
)-equation with initial data
\varphi_{\infty}(t=0)=\Phi_{0}
:

(1.38)

\varphi_{\infty}(t)=\Phi_{0}+\int_{0}^{t}{Q}_{0}(\varphi_{\infty}(s),\varphi_{\infty}(s))\hskip 1.0pt\mathrm{d}s.

In addition, there exists some positive constant
C>0
such that:

(1.39)

\forall t\in[0,T],\penalty\ \|\langle v\rangle^{m}(\varphi_{\hbar}(t)-\varphi_{\infty}(t))\|_{L^{2}}\leq C\hbar t.

Ideas of the proof

It will be useful to introduce the trilinear operator

(1.40)

Q_{\hbar}(g,h,\varpi)(v_{1}):=\tfrac{2}{\hbar^{2}}\int(g_{1}^{\prime}h_{2}^{\prime}-g_{1}h_{2})\varpi(k,v_{1}){\hat{\mathscr{V}}}(k)^{2}\hskip 1.0pt\mathrm{d}V

with
Q_{LB}^{\hbar}(\varphi)=Q_{\hbar}(\varphi,\varphi,\tfrac{1}{|\varepsilon_{\hbar}(\varphi)|^{2}})
.

The proof can be split into two steps. First, we construct solutions to (
QLB
) on short time interval (with length depending on
\hbar
), via a fix argument in a
H^{N}(\langle v\rangle^{m}\hskip 1.0pt\mathrm{d}v)
. The second step is the propagation of regularity on time scale independent of
\hbar
via an energy method.

As we want to understand the evolution of
\|\langle v\rangle^{m}\partial^{\alpha}_{v}\varphi_{\hbar}(t)\|^{2}_{L^{2}}
, we need to estimate

\displaystyle\left\langle\partial^{\alpha}_{v}\varphi_{\hbar},\partial_{v}^{\alpha}Q_{\hbar}(\varphi_{\hbar},\varphi_{\hbar},\tfrac{1}{|\varepsilon_{\hbar}(\varphi_{\hbar})|^{2}})\right\rangle=\sum_{\beta_{1}+\beta_{2}+\beta_{3}=\alpha}\binom{\alpha}{\beta_{1},\beta_{2},\beta_{3}}\left\langle\partial^{\alpha}_{v}\varphi_{\hbar},Q_{\hbar}\big(\partial_{v}^{\beta_{1}}\varphi_{\hbar},\partial_{v}^{\beta_{2}}\varphi_{\hbar},\partial_{v}^{\beta_{3}}\tfrac{1}{|\varepsilon_{\hbar}(\varphi_{\hbar})|^{2}}\big)\right\rangle

\displaystyle\text{with }\partial_{v}^{\beta_{3}}\tfrac{1}{|\varepsilon_{\hbar}(\varphi_{\hbar})|^{2}}=-2{\hat{\mathscr{V}}}(k)^{2}\Re\left(\tfrac{\overline{\varepsilon_{\hbar}(\varphi_{\hbar})}}{|\varepsilon_{\hbar}(\varphi_{\hbar})|^{4}}\int\tfrac{\partial_{v}^{\beta_{3}}\varphi_{\hbar}(v_{*})-\partial_{v}^{\beta_{3}}\varphi_{\hbar}(v_{*}-\hbar k)}{\hbar k\cdot(v_{*}-v-\hbar k)-i0}\hskip 1.0pt\mathrm{d}{v_{*}}\right)+\cdots

where the rest only implies lower order derivatives of
\varphi_{\hbar}
.

If
\max(|\beta_{1}|,|\beta_{2}|,|\beta_{3}|)\leq N-1
, we can use the naive estimation
\|g(\cdot)-g(\cdot+\hbar k)\|_{L^{2}}\leq\hbar|k|\|g\|_{H^{1}}
and usual Sobolev injection to bound
Q_{\hbar}\big(\partial_{v}^{\beta_{1}}\varphi_{\hbar},\partial_{v}^{\beta_{2}}\varphi_{\hbar},\partial_{v}^{\beta_{3}}\tfrac{1}{|\varepsilon_{\hbar}(\varphi_{\hbar})|^{2}}\big)
.

The more involving terms are
(\beta_{1},\beta_{2},\beta_{3})\in\{(\alpha,0,0),(0,\alpha,0),(0,0,\alpha)\}
. These terms need some additional quantitative regularity, in term of the following energy

\mathscr{A}_{\hbar,g}(f):=\int\tfrac{(f(v_{1}+\hbar k)-f(v_{1}))^{2}}{\hbar^{2}}g(v_{2})\hat{\mathscr{V}}(k)^{2}\delta_{k\cdot(v_{2}-v_{1}-\hbar k)}\hskip 1.0pt\mathrm{d}V.

We can bound
\mathscr{A}_{\hbar,\varphi_{\hbar}}(\partial^{\alpha}\varphi_{\hbar})
from the term
(\beta_{1},\beta_{2},\beta_{3})=(\alpha,0,0)
: using the symmetry
(v_{1},v_{2},k)\mapsto(v_{1}^{\prime},v_{2}^{\prime},-k)
,

\displaystyle\left\langle\partial^{\alpha}_{v}\varphi_{\hbar},Q_{\hbar}(\partial_{v}^{\alpha}\varphi_{\hbar},\varphi_{\hbar},\tfrac{1}{|\varepsilon_{\hbar}(\varphi_{\hbar})|^{2}})\right\rangle

\displaystyle=\tfrac{2}{\hbar^{2}}\int\partial_{v}^{\alpha}\varphi_{\hbar,1}(\partial_{v}^{\alpha}\varphi_{\hbar,1}^{\prime}\varphi_{\hbar,2}^{\prime}-\partial_{v}^{\alpha}\varphi_{\hbar,1}\varphi_{\hbar,2})\tfrac{{\hat{\mathscr{V}}}^{2}(k)}{|\varepsilon_{\hbar}(\varphi_{\hbar},k,v_{1})|^{2}}\delta_{k\cdot(v_{2}-v_{1}-\hbar k)}\hskip 1.0pt\mathrm{d}V

\displaystyle=\tfrac{2}{\hbar^{2}}\int\partial_{v}^{\alpha}\varphi_{\hbar,1}\varphi_{\hbar,2}(\partial_{v}^{\alpha}\varphi_{\hbar,1}^{\prime}-\partial_{v}^{\alpha}\varphi_{\hbar,1})\tfrac{{\hat{\mathscr{V}}}^{2}(k)}{|\varepsilon_{\hbar}(\varphi_{\hbar},k,v_{1})|^{2}}\delta_{k\cdot(v_{2}-v_{1}-\hbar k)}\hskip 1.0pt\mathrm{d}V

\displaystyle=-\tfrac{1}{\hbar^{2}}\int(\partial_{v}^{\alpha}\varphi_{\hbar,1}^{\prime}-\partial_{v}^{\alpha}\varphi_{\hbar,1})^{2}\varphi_{\hbar,2}\tfrac{{\hat{\mathscr{V}}}^{2}(k)}{|\varepsilon_{\hbar}(\varphi_{\hbar},k,v_{1})|^{2}}\delta_{k\cdot(v_{2}-v_{1}-\hbar k)}\hskip 1.0pt\mathrm{d}V

\displaystyle\penalty\ \penalty\ +\tfrac{1}{\hbar^{2}}\int((\partial_{v}^{\alpha}\varphi_{\hbar,1}^{\prime})^{2}-(\partial_{v}^{\alpha}\varphi_{\hbar,1})^{2})\varphi_{\hbar,2}\tfrac{{\hat{\mathscr{V}}}^{2}(k)}{|\varepsilon_{\hbar}(\varphi_{\hbar},k,v_{1})|^{2}}\delta_{k\cdot(v_{2}-v_{1}-\hbar k)}\hskip 1.0pt\mathrm{d}V.

The first term looks like
\mathscr{A}_{\hbar,\varphi_{\hbar}}(\partial^{\alpha}_{v}\varphi_{\hbar})
(as
\Phi_{0}
and
\tfrac{1}{|\varepsilon_{\hbar}(\varphi_{\hbar})|^{2}}\gtrsim 1
). The second term can be bound by the cancellation lemma (Propostion
5.7
). This estimation can be understood as a discrete integration by part

(1.41)

\frac{1}{\hbar^{2}}\int((\partial_{v}^{\alpha}\varphi_{\hbar,1}^{\prime})^{2}-(\partial_{v}^{\alpha}\varphi_{\hbar,1})^{2})\varphi_{\hbar,2}\tfrac{{\hat{\mathscr{V}}}^{2}(k)}{|\varepsilon_{\hbar}(\varphi_{\hbar},k,v_{1})|^{2}}\delta_{k\cdot(v_{2}-v_{1}-\hbar k)}\hskip 1.0pt\mathrm{d}V\\
=\int\partial_{v}^{\alpha}\varphi_{\hbar,1}^{2}\varphi_{\hbar,2}\left[\int\tfrac{{\hat{\mathscr{V}}}^{2}(k)}{\hbar^{2}}\left(\tfrac{\delta_{k\cdot(v_{2}-v_{1})}}{|\varepsilon_{\hbar}(\varphi_{\hbar},k,v_{1}-\hbar k)|^{2}}-\tfrac{\delta_{k\cdot(v_{2}-v_{1}-\hbar k)}}{|\varepsilon_{\hbar}(\varphi_{\hbar},k,v_{1})|^{2}}\right)\hskip 1.0pt{\rm{}^{-}\!\!\!\!d}k\right]\hskip 1.0pt\mathrm{d}v_{1}\hskip 1.0pt\mathrm{d}v_{2},

where the integral in
k
is bounded by
O(\tfrac{\langle v_{1}-v_{2}\rangle}{|v_{1}-v_{2}|^{3}})

1
1

1

Note that in the setting of the quantum Boltzmann equation presented in
[
HLPZ24
]
, the integral in
k
is bounded by some
\Psi(v_{2}-v_{1})
, with
\Psi\in L^{1}
. The authors use the symmetry
k\mapsto-k
of the collision kernel that leads exact cancellation. This symmetry is no longer verified in Lenard–Balescu setting.
, which is not
L^{1}_{\rm loc}
. Hence, this bound is only useful away from the singularitu, on
\{|{k}\cdot(v_{1}-v_{2})|>\tfrac{1}{K}\}
(with
K>1
an arbitrary constant).

In order to treat
\{|{k}\cdot(v_{1}-v_{2})|\leq\tfrac{1}{K}\}
, we use that

(1.42)

\frac{1}{\hbar^{2}}\int_{\{|{k}\cdot(v_{1}-v_{2})|\leq\tfrac{1}{K}\}}((\partial_{v}^{\alpha}\varphi_{\hbar,1}^{\prime})^{2}-(\partial_{v}^{\alpha}\varphi_{\hbar,1})^{2})\varphi_{\hbar,2}\tfrac{{\hat{\mathscr{V}}}^{2}(k)}{|\varepsilon_{\hbar}(\varphi_{\hbar},k,v_{1})|^{2}}\delta_{k\cdot(v_{2}-v_{1}-\hbar k)}\hskip 1.0pt\mathrm{d}V\\
=\frac{1}{2}\int_{\{|{k}\cdot(v_{1}-v_{2})|\leq\tfrac{1}{K}\}}\frac{(\partial_{v}^{\alpha}\varphi_{\hbar,1}^{\prime})^{2}-(\partial_{v}^{\alpha}\varphi_{\hbar,1})^{2}}{\hbar}\frac{\varphi_{\hbar,2}-\varphi_{\hbar,2}^{\prime}}{\hbar}\tfrac{{\hat{\mathscr{V}}}^{2}(k)}{|\varepsilon_{\hbar}(\varphi_{\hbar},k,v_{1})|^{2}}\delta_{k\cdot(v_{2}-v_{1}-\hbar k)}\hskip 1.0pt\mathrm{d}V

Then, we remark that (Proposition
5.2
), for
{\hat{\mathscr{V}}}
verifying Assumption
3
,
g\geq 0
and
\|\langle v\rangle^{\frac{d}{2}+4}(g-\Phi_{0})\|_{H^{1}}
small enough,

(1.43)

\|f\|_{L^{2}}^{2}+\mathscr{A}_{\hbar,g}(f)\simeq_{g}\|f\|_{L^{2}}^{2}+\int\tfrac{(f(v_{1}+\hbar k)-f(v_{1}))^{2}}{\hbar^{2}}\tfrac{1}{\langle k\rangle^{d+8}\langle v_{1}\rangle^{\frac{9}{2}}}\hskip 1.0pt\mathrm{d}v_{1}\hskip 1.0pt{\rm{}^{-}\!\!\!\!d}k.

This allows us to bound (
1.42
) .

2.
Validity at time
0
of the Quantum Lenard-Balescu equation

In the first part of the present paper, we present a system from which the quantum Lennard–Balescu equation can be derived (at least at time
0
).

The strategy is quiet disconnected from the rest of the paper, and is mainly inspired from
[
PPS19
]
for the estimation of the cumulant, and from
[
Due21
]
for the computation of the collision operator.

We denote
\mathscr{L}^{1}(\mathfrak{H})
the space of trace-class operator on the Hilbert space
\mathfrak{H}
, and
\mathfrak{H}_{L}:=L^{2}(\mathbb{T}_{L})
.

We recall that the system is describe by a density matrix
F_{N}(t)\in\mathscr{L}^{1}(\mathfrak{H}_{L}^{N})
, self-adjoint, symmetric: for all
\sigma\in\mathfrak{S}_{N}
and
(\psi_{i})_{i\leq N}\in\mathfrak{H}^{N}_{L}
,

\left\langle\psi_{1}\otimes\cdots\psi_{N},F_{N}(t)\psi_{1}\otimes\cdots\psi_{N}\right\rangle=\left\langle\psi_{\sigma(1)}\otimes\cdots\psi_{\sigma(N)},F_{N}(t)\psi_{\sigma(1)}\otimes\cdots\psi_{\sigma({N})}\right\rangle.

It is solution of the Von-Neuman–Fock equation

\frac{i\mu\hbar}{N}\partial_{t}F_{N}(t)=\frac{\mu\hbar^{2}}{N}\sum_{j=1}^{N}\left[-\triangle_{j},F_{N}(t)\right]+\frac{1}{N}\sum_{j<k}^{N}\left[V_{j,k},F_{N}(t)\right].

Here,
\triangle_{j}
is the Laplacian with respect to the
j
-th variable, and
V_{j,k}
the multiplication by
\mathscr{V}(x_{j}-x_{k})
.

At time
0
, we fix

\displaystyle F_{0,L}(x,y):=\left(\frac{2\pi\hbar}{L^{2}}\right)^{d}\frac{1}{\mathscr{Z}_{L}}\sum_{k\in\mathbb{Z}_{L}}\Phi_{0}(\hbar k)e^{ik\cdot(x-y)},

\displaystyle\hat{F}^{N}(t=0)=F_{0,L}^{\otimes N.}

2.1.
A priori
estimation of the cumulant

In the following we introduce the notations: for
f\in{\mathscr{L}}^{1}(\mathfrak{H}_{L})
and
\sigma\subset[1,N]

f^{\sigma}:=\bigotimes_{k\in\sigma}f_{k}\in{\mathscr{L}}^{1}(\mathfrak{H}^{\sigma}_{L}),

and
\tr_{\sigma}
the trace with respect the particles in
\sigma\subset[n]
:

\forall A\in{\mathscr{L}}^{1}(\mathfrak{H}^{N}),B\in{\mathscr{L}}(\mathfrak{H}^{\sigma}),\penalty\ \tr\left(\left(B\otimes\mathbbm{1}^{[1,n]\setminus\sigma}\right)A\right)=\tr\left(B\tr_{\sigma}A\right).

We use the notation
[m,n]=\{m,m+1,\cdots,n\}
and
[n]:=[1,n]
.

That defines the
k
-th marginal of the density matrix
F_{N}\in{\mathscr{L}}^{1}(\mathfrak{H}^{N})
:

F_{N}^{[k]}:=\tr_{[1,k]}F_{N}.

If the system start from a factorized initial data
F_{N,0}=F_{0}^{\otimes N}
, one can expect that the system remains almost factorized. As the interaction creates some correlation between particles it will never remains fully factorized and we introduce the family of density matrix
(G_{N,n})_{n}\in\prod_{n\geq 1}{\mathscr{L}}^{1}(\mathfrak{H}_{L}^{n})
to measure the defect of tensorisation:

(2.1)

G_{N}^{[n]}(t)=\sum_{\sigma\subset[n]}(-1)^{\sigma}G_{N}^{[n]\setminus\sigma}(t)\otimes(F_{N}^{(1)}(t))^{\sigma}.

Using the
G^{N}_{\omega}
, one can decompose the marginal as

(2.2)

F_{N}^{[n]}(t)=\sum_{\sigma\subset[1,n]}G_{N}^{[1,n]\setminus\sigma}(t)\otimes(F_{N}^{(1)}(t))^{\sigma}.

We will prove the following result, which is similar to Theorem 2.2 of
[
PPS19
]
.

Proposition 2.1
.

Let
(F_{0}^{N})\in\prod_{N}{\mathscr{L}}^{1}(\mathfrak{H}_{L}^{N})
a family of initial data such that for all
N\geq 1
,

(2.3)

\forall n\in[1,N],\|G_{N}^{[n]}(t=0)\|_{{\mathscr{L}}_{s}^{1}(\mathfrak{H}_{L}^{n})}\leq A^{n}\left(\frac{n}{\sqrt{N}}\right)^{n/2}

Then there exists a constant
C>0
independant of
C^{0}
such that for any
\beta\in(0,1/9)
,

\forall t>0\text{ such that }\frac{Nt}{\mu\hbar}\leq\frac{\beta\log N}{C\|\mathscr{V}\|_{\infty}},\penalty\ \forall n\geq 1,\penalty\ \|G_{N}^{[n]}(t)|\leq(CA)^{n}\left(\frac{n^{1-\beta}}{N^{\frac{1-3\beta}{2}}}\right)^{n}.

Proof.

We define
\tilde{F}(t)
the solution of the Hartree equation

\left\{\begin{split}\frac{iN\hbar}{\mu}\partial_{t}\tilde{F}(t)&=\frac{N\hbar^{2}}{\mu}\left[-\triangle,\tilde{F}(t)\right]+\tr_{1}\left[V_{1,2},\tilde{F}\otimes\tilde{F}(t)\right]\\
\tilde{F}(t=0)&=F_{N}^{(1)}(t=0)\end{split}\right.

We introduce the family of matrices
E_{[1,n]}^{N}(t)
defined by

(2.4)

E_{N}^{[n]}(t)=\sum_{\sigma\subset[n]}(-1)^{\sigma}F_{N}^{[n]\setminus\sigma}(t)\otimes(\tilde{F}(t))^{\sigma}.

It has been proved in
[
PPS19
]
that for
t>0
, there exists a constant
C>0
(independent of
N
and
L
) such that for all
t>0
,

(2.5)

\|F_{N}^{[1]}(t)-\tilde{F}(t)\|_{\mathscr{L}^{1}}\leq\frac{C}{N}e^{C\frac{tN}{\mu\hbar}},\penalty\ \|E^{\{1,2\}}_{N}(t)\|_{\mathscr{L}^{1}}\leq\frac{C}{N}e^{C\frac{tN}{\mu\hbar}}.

We deduce immediately that for
\beta\in(0,1)
,
\forall t
such that
\frac{tN}{\mu\hbar}\leq\frac{\beta\log N}{C\|\mathscr{V}\|_{\infty}}

\|G_{N}^{[2]}(t)\|_{\mathscr{L}^{1}}\leq\|\tilde{F}\otimes\tilde{F}-F_{N}^{\{1\}}F_{N}^{\{2\}}\|_{\mathscr{L}^{1}}+\|E_{N}^{[2]}\|_{\mathscr{L}^{1}}\leq\frac{C}{N}e^{C\frac{\|\mathscr{V}\|_{L^{\infty}}Nt}{\mu\hbar}}\leq\frac{C}{N^{1-\beta}}.

We can now look at the
G_{N}^{[n]}
. They are solution of the following hierarchy (its derivation is similar to the Appendix of
[
PPS19
]
)

(2.6)

\frac{i\hbar}{L^{d}}\partial_{t}G_{N}^{[n]}=\Bigg(\frac{\hbar^{2}}{L^{d}}K_{n}+\frac{1}{N}\sum_{j<k}T_{j,k}\Bigg)G_{N}^{[n]}+D_{n}({f_{1}})G_{N}^{[n]}+D^{1}_{n}({f_{1}})G_{N}^{[n+1]}\\[-5.0pt]
+\bar{D}^{-1}_{n}(F_{N}^{(1)},G_{N}^{[2]})G_{N}^{[n-1]}+D^{-2}_{n}(F_{N}^{[1]})G_{N}^{[n-2]}

where

\begin{array}[]{c}K_{n}:=\sum_{j=1}^{n}[-\tfrac{\triangle_{j}}{2},\cdot],\penalty\ T_{j,k}:=[V_{j,k},\cdot],\penalty\ C_{j,n+1}h_{[n+1]}=\tr_{[1,n]}\Big(T_{j,n+1}h_{[n+1]}\Big)\\[10.0pt]
\bar{D}_{n}^{-1}(f_{1},g_{2})h_{[n-1]}:=\sum_{j\in[n]}\Big(C_{1,2}g_{[2]}\Big)^{\{j\}}h_{[n]\setminus\{j\}}+D^{-1}_{n}(f_{1})h_{[n-1]}\\[15.0pt]
D^{1}_{N}=D_{0}^{-2}:=0\\[5.0pt]
D^{-1}_{1}(f_{1})h_{\emptyset}:=-\frac{1}{N}C_{1,2}f_{1}\otimes f_{1}\\[5.0pt]
D^{-2}_{2}(f_{1})h_{\emptyset}:=\frac{1}{N}\Big(T_{1,2}f_{1}\otimes f_{1}-\big(C_{1,2}(f_{1}\otimes f_{1})\big)\otimes f_{1}-f_{1}\otimes\big(C_{1,2}(f_{1}\otimes f_{1})\big)\Big)\end{array}

with the similar bound, for
\frac{Nt}{\mu\hbar}\leq\frac{\beta\log N}{C\|\mathscr{V}\|_{\infty}}
,
\beta\in(0,1/2)
,

\|\bar{D}^{-1}_{N}\|_{\mathscr{L}^{1}\to\mathscr{L}^{1}}\leq\|\mathscr{V}\|_{L^{\infty}}\Big(\frac{4n^{2}}{N}+n\|g_{[2]}\|_{\mathscr{L}^{1}}\Big)\leq\|\mathscr{V}\|_{L^{\infty}}\Big(\frac{4n^{2}}{N}+\frac{n}{N}Ce^{C\|\mathscr{V}\|_{\infty}\frac{tL^{d}}{\hbar}}\Big)\\
\leq C^{\prime}\|\mathscr{V}\|_{L^{\infty}}\Big(\frac{n^{2}}{N}+\frac{n}{N^{1-\beta}}\Big)\leq C^{\prime}\|\mathscr{V}\|_{\infty}\Big(\frac{n^{2}}{N}+\Big(\frac{n^{2}}{N}\Big)^{1-\beta}\Big).

Remark 2.1
.

Note that this hierarchy of equation can be simplified considering the
Grand canonical ensemble
: the number of particles is not fixed but is a random (see
[
PS21
]
).

In
[
PPS19
]
, the authors use the following technical lemma, which is proved in Section 3 of
[
PPS19
]

Lemma 2.2
.

Let
(g_{n}(t))_{n\leq N}
,
g_{n}:\mathbb{R}^{+}\to\mathscr{E}_{n}
be a sequence of function onto a family of Banach spaces
\mathscr{E}_{n}
, and
\alpha\in(0,1)
such that

\forall t\geq 0,\penalty\ \|g_{n}(t)\|_{\mathscr{E}_{n}}\leq 2^{n},\penalty\ \forall n\geq 1,\penalty\ \|g_{n}(0)\|_{\mathscr{E}_{n}}\leq A^{n}\Big(\frac{n^{2}}{N}\Big)^{\frac{\alpha n}{2}}

\partial_{t}\|h_{n}\|_{\mathscr{E}_{n}}\leq n\big(\|h_{n}\|_{\mathscr{E}_{n}}+\|h_{n+1}\|_{\mathscr{E}_{n+1}}\big)+\Big(\frac{n^{2}}{N}+\Big(\frac{n^{2}}{N}\Big)^{\alpha}\Big)\big(\|h_{n-1}\|_{\mathscr{E}_{n-1}}+\|h_{n-2}\|_{\mathscr{E}_{n-2}}\big)

Then there exists a constant
C
independent of
A
such that

\forall n\geq 1,\penalty\ \|g_{n}(t)\|_{\mathscr{E}_{n}}\leq\Big(CAe^{Ct}\Big)^{n}\Big(\frac{n^{2}}{N}\Big)^{\frac{\alpha n}{2}}.

This lemma was used in
[
PPS19
]
to prove estimation of (
2.5
)
2
2

2

Note that this lemma has not been explicitly written.
. Applying the lemma on the family
G^{[n]}_{N}(t)
, one obtain

\forall n\geq 1,\penalty\ \|G_{N}^{[n]}(t)\|_{\mathscr{L}^{1}}\leq\Big(CAe^{\frac{C\|\mathscr{V}\|_{L^{\infty}}Nt}{\mu\hbar}}\Big)^{n}\Big(\frac{n^{2}}{N}\Big)^{\frac{(1-\beta)n}{2}}\leq(CA)^{n}\left(\frac{n^{1-\beta}}{N^{\frac{1-3\beta}{2}}}\right)^{n}.

∎

2.2.
Effective equation for initial data stable by translation

In the classical setting, the Lenard-Balescu equation is a correction of the Vlasov dynamics in the case of uniformly distributed (in
x
) initial data. We have to precise the meaning in the quantum case.

Definition 2.1
.

We define for
z\in\mathbb{T}_{L}
the translation operator
\tau_{z}:\mathfrak{H}^{N}_{L}\to\mathfrak{H}^{N}_{L}
by

\forall\psi\in\mathfrak{H}^{N}_{L},\penalty\ (\tau_{z}\psi)(x_{1},\cdots,x_{N})=\psi(x_{1}+z,\cdots,x_{N}+z).

A density matrix
F^{N}\in{\mathscr{L}}^{1}(\mathfrak{H}_{L}^{N})
is said
invariant by translation
if it commutes with all the translation operators. We denote
{\mathscr{L}}_{i}^{1}(\mathfrak{H}_{L}^{N})\subset{\mathscr{L}}^{1}(\mathfrak{H}_{L}^{N})
the set of operator invariant by translation.

We can write this property for density matrix in the
X
variable: denoting
F^{N}(x_{1},\cdots,x_{N},y_{1},\cdots,y_{N})
its kernel,
F_{N}
is invariant by translation if

\forall z\in\mathbb{T}_{L},\penalty\ F_{N}(x_{1}+z,\cdots,x_{N}+z,y_{1}+z,\cdots,y_{N}+z)=F_{N}(x_{1},\cdots,x_{N},y_{1},\cdots,y_{N}).

This property can also be defined in the Fourier Variable:
Denote
\mathbb{Z}^{d}_{L}:=\left(\tfrac{2\pi}{L}\mathbb{Z}\right)^{d}
, and for
(k_{[N]},l_{[N]})\in\mathbb{Z}_{L}^{2nd}

\hat{F}_{N}(k_{[N]},l_{[N]}):=\iint F_{N}(x_{[N]},y_{[N]})e^{i(-k_{[N]}\cdot x_{[N]}+l_{[N]}\cdot y_{[N]})}dx_{[N]}dy_{[N]}.

Then
F^{N}
is invariant by translation if and only if

\sum_{j=1}^{N}k_{j}-l_{j}\neq 0\Rightarrow\hat{F}_{N}(k_{[N]},l_{[N]})=0.

Note that if
F_{N}
is invariant by translation, then for any
\omega\subset[N]
,
\tr_{\omega}F^{N}
is also invariant by translation.

As the Hamiltonian of the system commutes with all the translation,
F^{N}(t)
remains invariant by translation if the initial data is. In addition the function
x\mapsto f^{N}_{1}(t,x,x)
is constant and so the effective potential has no effect.

We can now compute the equations verified by
f^{N}_{1}
.

(2.7)

\begin{split}i\hbar\partial_{t}F_{N}^{(1)}&=[-\tfrac{\hbar^{2}}{2}\triangle,F_{N}^{[1]}]+\frac{N-1}{\mu}\tr_{1}[V_{1,2},F_{N}^{[2]}]=[-\tfrac{\hbar^{2}}{2}\triangle,F_{N}^{[1]}]+\frac{N-1}{\mu}\tr_{1}[V_{1,2},G_{N}^{[2]}].\end{split}

using that for denoting
H^{(n)}(X_{n},Y_{n})
the kernel of
H^{(n)}\in{\mathscr{L}}^{1}(\mathfrak{H}_{L}^{n})
, for
F\in{\mathscr{L}}^{1}(\mathfrak{H}_{L})
invariant by translation, and
\mathscr{V}
has spherical symmetry,
F(x,y)=\tilde{F}(x-y)
for some
\tilde{F}
and

(2.8)

\displaystyle[\triangle,F]=\triangle_{x}F(x-y)-\triangle_{y}F(x-y)=0,

(2.9)

\displaystyle\tr_{1}[V_{1,2},F\otimes F](x,y)=\int(\mathscr{V}(x-z)-\mathscr{V}(y-z))\tilde{F}(x-y)\tilde{F}(z-z)\hskip 1.0pt\mathrm{d}{z}=0.

To compute the equation verified by
G_{N}^{(2)}(t)
, we begin by derivating
F_{N}^{(2)}
. We denote

\begin{split}i\hbar\partial_{t}F_{N}^{[2]}=&[\tfrac{\hbar^{2}}{2}(-\triangle_{1}-\triangle_{2})+\frac{1}{\mu}V_{1,2},F_{N}^{[2]}]+\frac{N-2}{\mu}\tr_{12}[V_{1,3}+V_{2,3},F_{N}^{[3]}]\\
=&[\tfrac{\hbar^{2}}{2}(-\triangle_{1}-\triangle_{2})+\frac{1}{\mu}V_{1,2},G_{N}^{[2]}]+F_{N}^{\{1\}}[-\tfrac{\hbar^{2}}{2}\triangle_{2},F_{N}^{\{2\}}]+[-\tfrac{\hbar^{2}}{2}\triangle_{1},F_{N}^{\{1\}}]F_{N}^{\{2\}}\\
&+\frac{1}{\mu}[V_{1,2},F_{N}^{\{1\}}F_{N}^{\{2\}}]+\frac{N-2}{\mu}\Big(\tr_{12}[V_{2,3},G_{N}^{\{1,2\}}F_{N}^{\{3\}}]+\tr_{12}[V_{1,3},G^{\{1,2\}}_{N}F_{N}^{\{3\}}]\Big)\\
&+\frac{N-2}{\mu}\Big(F_{N}^{\{1\}}\tr_{2}\Big[V_{2,3},F_{N}^{\{2\}}F_{N}^{\{3\}}+G_{N}^{\{2,3\}}\Big]+\tr_{2}\Big[V_{1,3},F_{N}^{\{1\}}F_{N}^{\{3\}}+G_{N}^{\{1,3\}}\Big]F_{N}^{\{2\}}\Big)\\
&+\frac{N-2}{\mu}\Big(\tr_{12}[V_{2,3},G_{N}^{\{1,3\}}F_{N}^{\{2\}}]+\tr_{12}[V_{1,3},G_{N}^{\{2,3\}}F_{N}^{\{1\}}]+\tr_{12}[V_{1,3}+V_{2,3},G_{N}^{\{1,2,3\}}]\Big).\end{split}

Using that
\tr_{12}[V_{2,3},G_{N}^{\{1,2\}}F_{N}^{\{3\}}]
and
\tr_{12}[V_{1,3},G_{N}^{\{1,2\}}F_{N}^{\{3\}}]
vanish (in the same way that (
2.9
)),

\displaystyle i\hbar\partial_{t}G_{N}^{\{1,2\}}=

\displaystyle\Big[\tfrac{\hbar^{2}}{2}(-\triangle_{1}-\triangle_{2})+\frac{1}{\mu}V_{1,2},G_{N}^{\{1,2\}}\Big]+\frac{1}{\mu}\Big[V_{1,2},F_{N}^{\{1\}}F_{N}^{\{2\}}\Big]

\displaystyle+\frac{N-2}{\mu}\Big(\tr_{12}\Big[V_{1,3},g^{N}_{13}f^{N}_{2}\Big]+\tr_{12}\Big[V_{2,3},G_{N}^{\{1,3\}}F_{N}^{(2)}\Big]\Big)

\displaystyle-\frac{1}{\mu}\Big(F^{\{1\}}_{N}\tr_{2}\Big[V_{2,3},G_{N}^{\{2,3\}}\Big]+\tr_{1}\Big[V_{1,3},G_{N}^{\{1,3\}}\Big]F_{N}^{\{2\}}\Big)+\frac{N-2}{\mu}\tr_{12}\Big[V_{1,3}+V_{2,3},G_{N}^{\{1,2,3\}}\Big].

Consider a family of initial data
F^{N}_{0}\in{\mathscr{L}}_{s}^{1}(\mathfrak{H}^{N}_{L})
invariant by translation and such that the associated families
(G_{N}^{[n]}(t))_{n\leq N}
vanish at time
0
. Then, using the estimation on
G^{[2]}_{N}
and
G^{[3]}_{N}
obtained in Proposition
2.1
, we have that for
\tfrac{t}{\hbar}\leq\tfrac{\beta\log N}{C\|\mathscr{V}\|_{\infty}}
,
F_{N}^{[1]}
and
G_{N}^{[2]}
verify

(2.10)

\left\{\begin{split}i\hbar\mu\partial_{t}F_{N}^{[1]}&=\tr_{[1]}[V_{1,2},\mu G_{N}^{[2]}]+O_{{\mathscr{L}}^{1}}\left(N^{-1+\beta}\right),\\
i\hbar\partial_{t}\mu G_{N}^{[2]}&=[\hbar^{2}(-\triangle_{1}-\triangle_{2}),\mu G_{N}^{[2]}]+\tr_{[2]}\Big(\Big[V_{1,3},F_{N}^{\{1\}}G_{N}^{\{2,3\}}\Big]+\Big[V_{2,3},F_{N}^{\{2\}}G_{N}^{\{1,3\}}\Big]\Big)\\
&\hskip 170.71652pt+[V_{1,2},F_{N}^{\{1\}}F_{N}^{\{2\}}]+O_{{\mathscr{L}}^{1}}\left({N^{\frac{-1+9\beta}{2}}}\right).\end{split}\right.

Denoting
F^{L}_{0}:=F^{N}_{1}(0)
, we define
F_{\mu}^{L}:\mathbb{R}^{+}\to{\mathscr{L}}^{1}_{s}(\mathfrak{H}_{L})
and
G_{\mu}^{L}:\mathbb{R}^{+}\to{\mathscr{L}}^{1}_{s}(\mathfrak{H}^{2}_{L})
the solutions of the equation

(2.11)

\left\{\begin{array}[]{c}F_{\mu}^{L}(t=0)=F_{1}^{N}(0),\penalty\ G^{L}_{\mu}=0,\\[5.0pt]
i\hbar\mu\partial_{t}F_{\mu}^{L}=\tr_{1}[V_{1,2},G_{\mu}^{L}],\\[5.0pt]
\hfil\begin{split}i\hbar\partial_{t}G_{\mu}^{L}=[\tfrac{\hbar^{2}}{2}(-\triangle_{1}-\triangle_{2}),G_{\mu}^{L}]+\tr_{[2]}\Big(\Big[V_{1,3},F_{\mu}^{L,\{1\}}G_{\mu}^{L,\{2,3\}}\Big]+\Big[V_{2,3},F_{\mu}^{L,\{2\}}&G_{\mu}^{L,\{1,3\}}\Big]\Big)\\
&+[V_{1,2},F^{L}_{0}F^{L}_{0}].\end{split}\end{array}\right.

From a straight forward application Proposition
2.1
and Gronwall Lemma, we obtain

Proposition 2.3
.

For all
t
such that
\tfrac{Nt}{\mu\hbar}\leq\tfrac{\beta\log N}{C\|\mathscr{V}\|_{\infty}}
,

(2.12)

\|i\hbar\mu\partial_{t}(F_{\mu}^{L}(t)-F_{\mu}^{[1]}(t))\|_{\mathscr{L}^{1}(\mathfrak{H}_{L})}+\|G_{\mu}^{L}(t)-\mu G_{\mu}^{[2]}(t)\|_{\mathscr{L}^{1}(\mathfrak{H}^{2}_{L})}\leq O\left({N^{\frac{-1+9\beta}{2}}}\right).

2.3.
The Larg Box limit

In this section, we want to take the limit
L\to\infty
.

We begin by writing the equation (
2.11
) in the Fourier variables. As the matrix
F_{\mu}^{L}
and
G_{\mu}^{L}
are invariant by translation,

\hat{F}_{\mu}^{L}(k_{1},l_{1})=0\penalty\ {\rm if}\penalty\ k_{1}\neq l_{1},\penalty\ \hat{G}_{\mu}^{L}(k_{1},k_{2},l_{1},l_{2})=0\penalty\ {\rm if}\penalty\ k_{1}+k_{2}\neq l_{1}+l_{2}.

We introduce

(2.13)

\displaystyle\forall v_{1}\in\mathbb{Z}_{L/\hbar},\penalty\ f_{\mu,L}(t,v_{1}):=\frac{L^{d}}{(2\pi\hbar)^{d}}\hat{F}_{\mu}^{L}(t,\tfrac{v_{1}}{\hbar},-\tfrac{v_{1}}{\hbar}),

(2.14)

\displaystyle g_{\mu,L}(t,v_{1},v_{2},k):=\frac{L^{2d}}{(2\pi\hbar)^{2d}}\hat{G}_{\mu}^{L}(t,\tfrac{v_{1}}{\hbar},\tfrac{v_{2}}{\hbar},\tfrac{v_{1}}{\hbar}+k,\tfrac{v_{2}}{\hbar}-k).

Using that
\hat{F}_{\mu}^{L}
and
\hat{G}_{\mu}^{L}
are Hermitian and positive, one have

(2.15)

f_{\mu,L}(t,v_{1})\in\mathbb{R}^{+},\penalty\ g_{\mu,L}(t,v_{1},v_{2},k)=\overline{g_{\mu,L}(t,v_{1}+\hbar k,v_{2}-\hbar k,-k)}.

With the change of unknown, the initial data becomes

(2.16)

\forall v_{1}\in\mathbb{Z}^{d}_{L/\hbar},\penalty\ f_{0,L}(v_{1})=\frac{1}{\mathscr{Z}_{L}}f_{0}(v_{1}).

Proposition 2.4
.

Denoting for any function
\Delta_{k}h(v)=h(v)-h(v-\hbar k)
, and
E(v):=\frac{|v|^{2}}{2}
,

(2.17)

\left\{\begin{array}[]{c}\hbar\mu i\partial_{t}{f}_{\mu,L}(v_{1})=-2\penalty\ \frac{(2\pi\hbar)^{d}}{L^{2d}}\!\!\sum_{k\in\mathbb{Z}^{d}_{L},\,v_{2}\in\mathbb{Z}^{d}_{L/\hbar}}{{\hat{\mathscr{V}}}}(k)({g}_{\mu,L}(v_{1},v_{2},k)-{g}_{\mu,L}(v_{1}+\hbar k,v_{2}-\hbar k,-k)),\\[16.0pt]
\hbar\partial_{t}{g}_{\mu,L}(t)+i(K_{2}+B^{L}){g}_{\mu,L}(t)=iA^{L}\\[7.0pt]
A^{L}(v_{1},v_{2},k):={\hat{\mathscr{V}}}(k)({f}_{0,L}(v_{1}){f}_{0,L}(v_{2})-{f}_{0,L}(v_{1}+\hbar k){f}_{0,L}(v_{2}-\hbar k))\end{array}\right.

where
K_{2}
is the kinetic part, an unbounded multiplicative operator

(2.18)

K_{2}g(v_{1},v_{2},k):=\big(\Delta_{-k}E(v_{1})+\Delta_{k}E(v_{2})\big)g(v_{1},v_{2},k)

and
B^{L}
is a bounded operator on (note that it is even compact)

(2.19)

B^{L}g(v_{1},v_{2},k):={{\hat{\mathscr{V}}}}(k)\Bigg(\Delta_{-k}f_{0,L}(v_{1})\frac{(2\pi)^{d}}{\hbar^{d}L^{d}}\sum_{\tilde{v}_{1}\in\mathbb{Z}_{L/\hbar}}g(\tilde{v}_{1},v_{2},k)+\Delta_{k}f_{0,L}(v_{2})\frac{(2\pi)^{d}}{\hbar^{d}L^{d}}\sum_{\tilde{v}_{2}\in\mathbb{Z}_{L/\hbar}}g(v_{1},\tilde{v}_{2},k)\Bigg)

Proof.

We recall that

\displaystyle i\hbar\mu\partial_{t}F_{\mu}^{L}=\tr_{1}[V_{1,2},G_{\mu}^{L}],

\displaystyle\begin{split}i\hbar\partial_{t}G_{\mu}^{L}=[\tfrac{\hbar^{2}}{2}(-\Delta_{1}-\Delta_{2}),G_{\mu}^{L}]+\tr_{[2]}\Big(\Big[V_{1,3},F_{\mu}^{L,\{1\}}G_{\mu}^{L,\{2,3\}}\Big]+\Big[V_{2,3},F_{\mu}^{L,\{2\}}&G_{\mu}^{L,\{1,3\}}\Big]\Big)+[V_{1,2},F^{L}_{0}F^{L}_{0}].\end{split}

We set
\Psi:=[V_{1,2},G_{\mu}^{L}]
. Its kernel is

\displaystyle\Psi

\displaystyle(x,y)=\int_{\mathbb{T}_{L}}\left[\mathscr{V}(x-z)-\mathscr{V}(z-y)\right]{G}_{\mu}^{L}(x,z,y,z)\hskip 1.0pt\mathrm{d}{z}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{L^{5d}}\sum_{k,\ell_{1},\ell_{2},p_{1},p_{2}\in\mathbb{Z}^{d}_{L}}\int_{\mathbb{T}_{L}}\left[e^{i(k(x-z)+\ell_{1}x+\ell_{2}z-p_{1}y-p_{2}z)}-e^{i(k(z-y)+\ell_{1}x+\ell_{2}z-p_{1}y-p_{2}z)}\right]{\hat{\mathscr{V}}}(k)\hat{G}_{\mu}^{L}(\ell_{1},\ell_{2},p_{1},q_{2})\hskip 1.0pt\mathrm{d}{z}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{L^{4d}}\sum_{k,\ell_{1},\ell_{2},p_{1}\in\mathbb{Z}^{d}_{L}}\left[e^{i((k+\ell_{1})x-p_{1}y)}\hat{G}_{\mu}^{L}(\ell_{1},\ell_{2},p_{1},\ell_{2}-k)-e^{i(\ell_{1}x-(k+p_{1})y)}\hat{G}_{\mu}^{L}(\ell_{1},\ell_{2},p_{1},p_{1}+k)\right]{\hat{\mathscr{V}}}(k)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{L^{4d}}\sum_{k,\ell_{1},\ell_{2},p_{1}\in\mathbb{Z}^{d}_{L}}\left[\hat{G}_{\mu}^{L}(\ell_{1}-k,\ell_{2},p_{1},\ell_{2}-k)-\hat{G}_{N}^{L}(\ell_{1},\ell_{2},p_{1}-k,\ell_{2}+k)\right]{\hat{\mathscr{V}}}(k)e^{i(\ell_{1}x-p_{1}y)}.

We deduce that

\hat{\Psi}(\ell_{1},\ell_{1})=\frac{1}{L^{2d}}\sum_{k,\ell_{2}\in\mathbb{Z}^{d}_{L}}\left[\hat{G}_{N}^{L}(\ell_{1}+k,\ell_{2}-k,\ell_{1},\ell_{2})-\hat{G}_{\mu}^{L}(\ell_{1},\ell_{2},\ell_{1}+k,\ell_{2}-k)\right]{\hat{\mathscr{V}}}(k)

This gives the evolution equation of
f_{\mu,L}
. The evolution equation of
g_{\mu,L}
can be computed in the same way.
∎

We define the couple
(f_{\mu},g_{\mu})
on
\mathbb{R}^{d}
and
\mathbb{R}^{3d}
as the solution of the equation

(2.20)

\left\{\begin{array}[]{c}\hbar N\partial_{t}{f}_{\mu}(v_{1})=-4\Im\penalty\ \int\hskip 1.0pt{\rm{}^{-}\!\!\!\!d}k\hskip 1.0pt\mathrm{d}v_{2}{\hat{\mathscr{V}}}(k){g}_{\mu}(v_{1},v_{2},k),\\[10.0pt]
\hbar\partial_{t}{g}_{\mu}(t)+i(K_{2}+B){g}_{\mu}(t)=iA,\\[7.0pt]
{f}_{\mu}(t=0)=\Phi_{0},\penalty\ {g}_{\mu}(t=0)=0,,\end{array}\right.

where we denote

(2.21)

\displaystyle A(v_{1},v_{2},k)

\displaystyle:={\hat{\mathscr{V}}}(k)(f_{0}(v_{1})f_{0}(v_{2})-f_{0}(v_{1}+\hbar k)f_{0}(v_{2}-\hbar k)),

(2.22)

\displaystyle Bh(v_{1},v_{2},k)

\displaystyle:={{\hat{\mathscr{V}}}(k)}\Bigg(\Delta_{-k}f_{0}(k_{1})\int\hskip 1.0pt\mathrm{d}\tilde{v}_{1}h(\tilde{v}_{1},v_{2},k)+\Delta_{k}f_{0}(v_{2})\int d\tilde{v}_{1}h(v_{1},\tilde{v}_{2},k)\Bigg).

We want to compare the
({f}_{\mu,L}(t),{g}_{\mu,L}(t))
with
({f}_{\mu}(t),{g}_{\mu}(t))
. The space
{\mathscr{L}}^{1}(\mathfrak{H}_{L})
and
{\mathscr{L}}^{1}(\mathfrak{H}^{2d}_{L})
can be injected respectively in
\ell^{1}_{v_{1}}(\mathbb{Z}^{d}_{L/\hbar})
and
\ell_{k}^{\infty}(\mathbb{Z}^{d}_{L},\ell^{1}_{v_{1},v_{2}}(\mathbb{Z}^{2}_{L/\hbar}))
(see Section 1.5 of
[
Gol13
]
):

\displaystyle\|f\|_{\ell_{v_{1}}^{1}}:=\frac{(2\pi\hbar)^{d}}{L^{d}}\sum_{v_{1}\in\mathbb{Z}^{d}_{L/\hbar}}|{f}(v_{1})|\leq\left\|F\right\|_{{\mathscr{L}}^{1}_{s,i}(\mathfrak{H}_{L})},

\displaystyle\|g\|_{\ell^{\infty}_{k}(\ell_{v_{1},v_{2}}^{1})}:=\sup_{k\in\mathbb{Z}^{d}_{L}}\frac{(2\pi\hbar)^{2d}}{L^{2d}}\sum_{v_{1},v_{2}\in\mathbb{Z}^{d}_{L/\hbar}}|\hat{g}(v_{1},v_{2},k)|\leq\|G\|_{{\mathscr{L}}^{1}_{s,i}(\mathfrak{H}^{2}_{L})}.

We want to find two Banach spaces,
\mathscr{E}_{1}
containing both
L^{1}_{v_{1}}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
and the
\ell^{1}_{v_{1}}(\mathbb{Z}^{d}_{L/\hbar})
for any
L
, and
\mathscr{E}_{2}
containing both
L^{\infty}_{k}(\mathbb{R}^{d},L^{1}_{v_{1},v_{2}}(\mathbb{R}^{2d}))
and the
\ell_{k}^{\infty}(\mathbb{Z}_{L},\ell^{1}_{v_{1},v_{2}}(\mathbb{Z}^{2d}_{L/\hbar}))
for any
L
. For
\mathscr{E}_{1}
we take the set of bounded radon measure on
\mathbb{R}^{d}

with total variation norm, with injection

\begin{array}[]{c}\pi^{\infty}_{1}:f\in L^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{d})\mapsto f(v_{1})\hskip 1.0pt\mathrm{d}v_{1},\penalty\ \penalty\ \pi^{L}_{1}:f^{L}\in\ell^{1}(\mathbb{Z}_{L})\mapsto\frac{(2\pi\hbar)^{d}}{L^{d}}\sum_{v_{1}\in\mathbb{Z}^{d}_{L/\hbar}}f^{L}(v_{1})\delta_{v_{1}}.\end{array}

For
\mathscr{E}_{2}
we consider the space of radon measure
h
on
\mathbb{R}^{3d}
such that the norm

\|h\|_{\mathscr{E}_{2}}:=\sup_{\begin{subarray}{c}k\in\mathbb{R}^{d}\\
\varepsilon>0\end{subarray}}\frac{1}{|B_{k}(\varepsilon)|}\|h(v_{1},v_{2},\tilde{k})\|_{TV((B_{k}(\varepsilon))_{k}\times\mathbb{R}^{d}_{v_{1}}\times\mathbb{R}^{d}_{v_{2}})}

is finite (where
|B_{k}(\varepsilon)|:=\{k^{\prime}\text{ with }|k^{\prime}-k|<\varepsilon\}
), with the injection

\displaystyle g\in L^{\infty}_{k}(L^{1}_{v_{1},v_{2}})\mapsto g(k,v_{1},v_{2})\hskip 1.0pt{\rm{}^{-}\!\!\!\!d}{k}\hskip 1.0pt\mathrm{d}{v_{1}}\hskip 1.0pt\mathrm{d}{v_{2}},\penalty\

\displaystyle g^{L}\in\ell_{k}^{\infty}(\ell^{1}_{v_{1},v_{2}}(\mathbb{Z}^{3}_{L})\mapsto\frac{(2\pi\hbar)^{2d}}{L^{3d}}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}v_{1},v_{2}\in\mathbb{Z}^{d}_{L/\hbar},\\
k\in\mathbb{Z}^{d}_{L}\end{subarray}}g^{L}(v_{1},v_{2},k)\delta_{v_{1},v_{2},k}.

Denoting, for
h(v_{1},v_{2},k)
a radon measure on
\mathbb{R}^{3d}
, (here the brackets designate the duality product)

\left<h(v_{1},v_{2},k)\right>_{v_{1}}:(v_{2},k)\mapsto\left<h(v_{1},v_{2},k),1_{v_{1}}\right>_{v_{1}}.

One can now extend the operator
B:L_{k}^{\infty}(L^{1}_{v_{1},v_{2}})\to\ell^{1}_{v_{1}}(\mathbb{Z}^{d}_{L/\hbar})
and
B^{L}:\ell_{k}^{\infty}(\ell^{1}_{v_{1},v_{2}})\to\ell^{1}_{v_{1}}(\mathbb{Z}^{d}_{L/\hbar})
as operators
\mathscr{E}_{2}\to\mathscr{E}_{1}
by

\displaystyle B^{L}h(v_{1},v_{2},k):={\hat{\mathscr{V}}}(k)\bigg(\pi_{1}^{L}\left(\Delta_{-k}f^{L}_{0}\right)\!\!(v_{1})\left<h(\tilde{v}_{1},v_{2},k)\right>_{\tilde{v}_{1}}+\pi_{1}^{L}\left(\Delta_{k}f_{0}^{L}\right)\!\!(v_{2})\left<h(v_{1},\tilde{v}_{2},k)\right>_{\tilde{v}_{2}}\bigg)

\displaystyle Bh(v_{1},v_{2},k):={\hat{\mathscr{V}}}(k)\bigg(\pi_{1}^{\infty}\left(\Delta_{-k}f_{0}\right)\!\!(v_{1})\left<h(\tilde{v}_{1},v_{2},k)\right>_{\tilde{v}_{1}}+\pi_{1}^{\infty}\left(\Delta_{k}f_{0}\right)\!\!(v_{2})\left<h(v_{1},\tilde{v}_{2},k)\right>_{\tilde{v}_{2}}\bigg)

Proposition 2.5
.

Fix an initial data
f_{0}
with
\|\langle v\rangle^{d+1}\langle\nabla\rangle f_{0}\|_{L^{\infty}}<\infty
.

For any parameter
N,\hbar
and
L
,

\mu\hbar\|\partial_{t}(\pi^{\infty}_{1}{f}_{\mu})-\pi^{L}_{1}f_{\mu,L}\|_{TV}\lesssim\|\pi_{2}^{\infty}g_{\mu}(t)-\pi_{2}^{L}g_{\mu,L}(t)\|_{\mathscr{E}_{2}}\lesssim\frac{\|\langle v\rangle^{d+1}\langle\nabla\rangle f_{0}\|_{L^{\infty}}}{L/\hbar}e^{\frac{4\|{\hat{\mathscr{V}}}\|_{L^{\infty}}t}{\hbar}}.

Proof.

We begin by the following lemma which can be directly deduced from the definition of the operator
B
and
B^{L}
.

Lemma 2.6
.

The operator
B
and
B^{L}
are bounded in
\mathscr{E}_{2}\to\mathscr{E}_{2}
, with the following bound

\|B\|_{\mathscr{E}_{2}\to\mathscr{E}_{2}}\leq\frac{4}{(2\pi)^{d}}\|{\hat{\mathscr{V}}}\|_{L^{\infty}}\|f_{0}\|_{TV},\penalty\ \|B^{L}\|_{\mathscr{E}_{2}\to\mathscr{E}_{2}}\leq\frac{4}{(2\pi)^{d}}\|{\hat{\mathscr{V}}}\|_{L^{\infty}}\|f^{L}_{0}\|_{TV},

\|B-B^{L}\|_{\mathscr{E}_{2}\to\mathscr{E}_{2}}\leq\frac{4}{(2\pi)^{d}}\|{\hat{\mathscr{V}}}\|_{L^{\infty}}\|f_{0}-f^{L}_{0}\|_{TV}.

We have

\partial_{t}(g_{\mu,L}-g_{\mu})+iK_{2}(g_{\mu,L}-g_{\mu})=-iB^{L}(g_{\mu,L}-g_{L})-i(B-B^{L})g_{\mu}+i(A-A^{L}).

Using that
iK_{2}
is the generator of an unitary group on
\mathscr{E}_{2}
and that
\|f\|_{TV}=\|f^{L}\|_{TV}=1
(we begin with two probability measure),

\|A-A^{L}\|_{\mathscr{E}_{2}}\leq 4\|{\hat{\mathscr{V}}}\|_{L^{\infty}}\|\pi_{1}^{\infty}f^{0}-\pi_{1}^{L}f_{0}^{L}\|_{TV},

we have by a Gronwall lemma

\|\pi_{2}^{\infty}g_{\mu}(t)-\pi_{2}^{L}g_{\mu,L}(t)\|_{\mathscr{E}_{2}}\leq\|\pi_{1}^{\infty}f^{0}-\pi_{1}^{L}f_{0}^{L}\|_{TV}\exp\left({\frac{4\|V\|_{L^{1}}t}{(2\pi)^{d}\hbar}}\right).

Because
\mathscr{V}
verifies Assumption
2
,

\mu\hbar\|\partial_{t}(\pi^{\infty}_{1}{f}_{\mu}-\pi^{L}_{1}f_{\mu,L})\|_{TV}\lesssim\|\pi_{2}^{\infty}g_{\mu}(t)-\pi_{2}^{L}g_{\mu,L}(t)\|_{\mathscr{E}_{2}}\lesssim\|\pi_{1}^{\infty}f^{0}-\pi_{1}^{L}f_{0}^{L}\|_{TV}\exp\left({\frac{4\|\mathscr{V}\|_{L^{1}}t}{(2\pi)^{d}\hbar}}\right).

We need to
\|\pi_{1}^{\infty}f^{0}-\pi_{1}^{L}f_{0}^{L}\|_{TV}
. We can decompose
\mathscr{Z}_{L}
as

\mathscr{Z}_{L}=1+\frac{(2\pi\hbar)^{d}}{L^{d}}\sum_{v\in\mathbb{Z}^{d}_{L/\hbar}}\left(f_{0}(v)-\int_{[-\frac{1}{2},\frac{1}{2}]^{d}}f_{0}(v+\tfrac{2\pi}{L/\hbar}\ell)d\ell\right)

and finally,

\displaystyle\|\pi_{2}^{\infty}g_{\mu}(t)-\pi_{2}^{L}g_{\mu,L}(t)\|_{\mathscr{E}_{2}}

\displaystyle=\frac{(2\pi\hbar)^{d}}{L^{d}}\sum_{v\in\mathbb{Z}^{d}_{L/\hbar}}\left|\frac{f_{0}(v)}{\mathscr{Z}_{L}}-\int_{[-\frac{1}{2},\frac{1}{2}]^{d}}f_{0}(v+\tfrac{2\pi}{L/\hbar}w)\hskip 1.0pt\mathrm{d}w\right|

\displaystyle\leq\left|1-\frac{1}{\mathscr{Z}_{L}}\right|+\frac{(2\pi\hbar)^{d}}{L^{d}}\sum_{v\in\mathbb{Z}^{d}_{L/\hbar}}\left|f_{0}(v)-\int_{[-\frac{1}{2},\frac{1}{2}]^{d}}f_{0}(v+\tfrac{2\pi}{L/\hbar}w)\hskip 1.0pt\mathrm{d}{w}\right|

\displaystyle\lesssim\frac{\hbar^{d}}{L^{d}}\sum_{v\in\mathbb{Z}^{d}_{L/\hbar}}\frac{\|\langle x\rangle^{d+1}\langle\nabla\rangle f_{0}\|_{L^{\infty}}}{L/\hbar\langle v\rangle^{d+1}}\lesssim\frac{\|\langle x\rangle^{d+1}\langle\nabla\rangle f_{0}\|_{L^{\infty}}}{L/\hbar}.

∎

2.4.
Computation of the collision term

In this final section, we study the limit of the
(f_{\mu},g_{\mu})
as
\mu\to\infty
. The first step is to apply the Laplace transform in time

Proposition 2.7
.

For any smooth test function
\varphi\in\mathscr{C}^{\infty}_{c}(\mathbb{R}^{+}\times\mathbb{R}^{d})
,

(2.23)

\mu\int_{0}^{\infty}\left<\partial_{t}\tilde{f}_{\mu}(\tau_{N}t,v_{1})\varphi(t,v_{1})\right>_{\!\!v_{1}}\!\!\hskip 1.0pt\mathrm{d}{t}\\
=\int_{\mathbb{R}}\frac{\hskip 1.0pt\mathrm{d}{\alpha}}{i\pi\hbar}\left<\frac{\tilde{\varphi}(\alpha,v_{1})}{1+i\alpha}\left(T\left(\tfrac{\hbar(i-\alpha)}{\tau_{N}},k,v_{1}\right)-T\left(\tfrac{\hbar(i-\alpha)}{\tau_{N}},-k,v^{\prime}_{1}\right)\right)\right\rangle_{\!\!k,v_{1}}

where
\tilde{\varphi}(\alpha,v_{1})
is the Laplace transform in time of
\varphi
(with
\langle f(k)\rangle_{k}:=\int f(k)\hskip 1.0pt{\rm{}^{-}\!\!\!\!d}k
).

\tilde{\varphi}(\alpha,v_{1}):=\frac{1}{2\pi}\int_{0}^{\infty}e^{t(1+i\alpha)}\varphi(t,v_{1})\hskip 1.0pt\mathrm{d}{t}

and

(2.24)

T(\omega,k,v_{1}):={\hat{\mathscr{V}}}(k)\left<\left((K_{2}+B)-\omega\right)^{-1}A(v_{1},v_{2},k)\right>_{\!\!v_{2}}.

Note that the preceding proposition is justified by the existence of the resolvent
\left(K_{2}+B-\frac{\hbar}{\tau_{N}}(i-\alpha)\right)^{-1}
.

Proof.

The proof is the same than Lemma 5.2 of
[
DSR21
]
.

We recall that
g_{N}
is solution of

\displaystyle\hbar\partial_{t}{g}_{\mu}(t)+i(K_{2}+B){g}_{\mu}(t)=iA

\displaystyle{g}_{\mu}(t=0)=0.

Using the Duhamel form of the evolution equation of
g_{\mu}
, and that
\delta(t)=\frac{1}{2\pi}\int e^{i\alpha t}\hskip 1.0pt\mathrm{d}{\alpha}
in the sense of distribution,

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{\infty}g_{\mu}(\tau_{N}t)\varphi(t)\hskip 1.0pt\mathrm{d}{t}

\displaystyle=\frac{i}{\hbar}\int_{0}^{\infty}\int_{0}^{\tau_{N}t}e^{-\frac{i}{\hbar}(K_{2}+B)(t-t_{1})}A\hskip 1.0pt\mathrm{d}{t_{1}}\penalty\ \varphi(t)\hskip 1.0pt\mathrm{d}{t}

\displaystyle=\frac{i}{\hbar}\int_{(\mathbb{R}^{+})^{3}}e^{-(\frac{1}{\tau_{N}}+\frac{i}{\hbar}(K_{2}+B))t_{2}}Ae^{-\frac{t_{2}}{\tau_{N}}}e^{t}\varphi(t)\delta_{t_{1}+t_{2}=\tau_{N}t}\hskip 1.0pt\mathrm{d}{t_{1}}\hskip 1.0pt\mathrm{d}{t_{2}}\hskip 1.0pt\mathrm{d}{t}

\displaystyle=\frac{i}{2\pi\hbar\tau_{N}}\int_{\mathbb{R}}\int_{(\mathbb{R}^{+})^{3}}e^{-(\frac{1+i\alpha}{\tau_{N}}+\frac{i}{\hbar}(K_{2}+B))t_{2}}Ae^{-\frac{1+i\alpha}{\hbar\tau_{N}}t_{1}}e^{(1+i{\alpha})t}\varphi(t)\hskip 1.0pt\mathrm{d}{t_{1}}\hskip 1.0pt\mathrm{d}{t_{2}}\hskip 1.0pt\mathrm{d}{t}\hskip 1.0pt\mathrm{d}{\alpha}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2\pi}\int_{\mathbb{R}}\left(\frac{\hbar}{\tau_{N}}(\alpha-i)+(K_{2}+B)\right)^{\!\!\!-1}\hskip-7.0ptA\penalty\ \penalty\ \frac{\tilde{\varphi}(\alpha)}{1+i\alpha}\hskip 1.0pt\mathrm{d}{\alpha}

Using the symmetry property of
g_{\mu}(t,v_{1},v_{2},k)
, one has

\mu\partial_{t}{f}_{\mu}(v_{1})=-\frac{2}{i\hbar}\penalty\ \left<{\hat{\mathscr{V}}}(k)\left({g}_{\mu}(v_{1},v_{2},k)-{g}_{\mu}(v^{\prime}_{1},v^{\prime}_{2},-k)\right)\right>_{v_{2},k},

where
v_{1}^{\prime}:=v_{1}+\hbar k
and
v_{2}^{\prime}:=v_{2}-\hbar k
.

The result is obtain by the combination of the both identities.
∎

Proposition 2.8
.

The function
T(\omega,k,v_{1})
verifies the following bounds and convergence results: for any
\omega\in\mathbb{C}\setminus\mathbb{R}
,

(2.25)

\displaystyle\left|T(\omega,k,v_{1})-T(\omega,-k,v^{\prime}_{1})\right|\lesssim{{\hat{\mathscr{V}}}}^{2}(k)\|\langle v\rangle^{2}\langle\nabla\rangle^{2}f_{0}\|_{L^{\infty}}^{2}\log\left(1+\frac{|\Re\omega|}{|\Im\omega|}\right)^{2},

(2.26)

\displaystyle\lim_{\begin{subarray}{c}\omega\to 0\\
\Im\omega>|\Re\omega|\end{subarray}}\frac{T(\omega,k,v_{1})-T(\omega,-k,v^{\prime}_{1})}{2\pi i}=\frac{{\hat{\mathscr{V}}}^{2}(k)\left<(f_{0}(v_{2}^{\prime})f_{0}(v_{1}^{\prime})-f_{0}(v_{1})f_{0}(v_{2}))\delta_{\Delta_{k}E(v_{2})+\Delta_{-k}E(v_{1})}\right>_{v_{2}}}{(2\pi)^{d}|\tilde{\varepsilon}(k,i0-\Delta_{-k}E(v_{1}))|^{2}},

Proof.

We decompose
(K_{2}+B)
as
L_{1}+L_{2}
, where
L_{1}
,
L_{2}
are defined by

(2.27)

\displaystyle L_{1}h(v_{1},v_{2},k)

\displaystyle:=\Delta_{-k}E(v_{1})h(v_{1},v_{2},k)+{{\hat{\mathscr{V}}}(k)}\Delta_{-k}f_{0}(v_{1})\left<h(\tilde{v}_{1},v_{2},k)\right>_{\tilde{k}_{1}}

(2.28)

\displaystyle L_{2}h(v_{1},v_{2},k)

\displaystyle:=\Delta_{k}E(v_{2})h(v_{1},v_{2},k)+{\hat{\mathscr{V}}}(k)\Delta_{k}f_{0}(v_{2})\left<h(v_{1},\tilde{v}_{2},k)\right>_{\tilde{k}_{2}}.

The operators
L_{1}
and
L_{2}
are easier to study than
K_{2}+B
, which can be recover by

(2.29)

((K_{2}+B^{L})-\omega)^{-1}=\frac{1}{2\pi i}\int(L_{2}+\beta-\frac{\omega}{2})^{-1}(L_{2}-\beta-\frac{\omega}{2})^{-1}\hskip 1.0pt\mathrm{d}{\beta}

The resolvant
L_{1}
and
L_{2}
are given by

(2.30)

\displaystyle(L_{1}-\omega)^{-1}h

\displaystyle=\frac{h}{\Delta_{-k}E(v_{1})-\omega}-\frac{{{\hat{\mathscr{V}}}}(k)}{\tilde{\varepsilon}(-k,\omega)}\frac{\Delta_{-k}f_{0}(v_{1})}{\Delta_{-k}E(v_{1})-\omega}\left<\frac{h}{\Delta_{-k}E(\tilde{v}_{1})-\omega}\right>_{\tilde{v}_{1}}

(2.31)

\displaystyle(L_{2}-\omega)^{-1}h

\displaystyle=\frac{h}{\Delta_{k}E(v_{2})-\omega}-\frac{{{\hat{\mathscr{V}}}}(k)}{\tilde{\varepsilon}(k,\omega)}\frac{\Delta_{k}f_{0}(v_{2})}{\Delta_{k}E(v_{2})-\omega}\left<\frac{h}{\Delta_{k}E(\tilde{v}_{2})-\omega}\right>_{\tilde{v}_{2}}.

where the (modified) dielectric constant
\tilde{\varepsilon}
is defined by

(2.32)

\tilde{\varepsilon}(k,\omega):=1+{{\hat{\mathscr{V}}}(k)}\left<\frac{\Delta_{k}f_{0}(k_{*})}{\Delta_{k}E(v_{*})-\omega}\right>_{v_{*}}=1+{{\hat{\mathscr{V}}}(k)}\int\frac{f_{0}(k_{*})-f_{0}(v_{*}-\hbar k)}{\hbar k\cdot\left(v_{*}-\frac{\hbar k}{2}\right)-\omega}\hskip 1.0pt\mathrm{d}{v_{*}}

First we give an estimation of
\tilde{\varepsilon}

Lemma 2.9
.

There exists a constant
C>0
such that if
f_{0}
satifies the bound
\|\mathscr{V}\|_{L^{1}}\|\langle v\rangle^{2d}\langle\nabla\rangle^{2}f_{0}\|_{L^{\infty}}<1/C
,
the function
\omega\mapsto\tilde{\varepsilon}(k,\omega)
is holomorphic on
\{\omega,\,\Im\omega\neq 0\}
and there exist a constant
\delta\in(0,1)
independent of
\omega
such that

|\tilde{\varepsilon}(k,\omega)|>\delta
. In addition,

(2.33)

|1-\tilde{\varepsilon}(k,\omega)|\lesssim\left\{\begin{split}&{\|\langle v\rangle^{2d}\langle\nabla\rangle^{2}f_{0}\|_{L^{\infty}}/|\Im\omega|},\\
&{\|\langle v\rangle^{2d}\langle\nabla\rangle^{2}f_{0}\|_{L^{\infty}}}/{|\Re\omega|}\penalty\ {\rm if}\penalty\ |\Re\omega|>4\hbar^{2}|k|^{2},\end{split}\right.

and in the same way

(2.34)

|\tfrac{\partial}{\partial\Re\omega}\tilde{\varepsilon}(k,\omega)|\lesssim\left\{\begin{split}&{\|\langle v\rangle^{2d}\langle\nabla\rangle^{3}f_{0}\|_{L^{\infty}}/|\Im\omega\hbar k|},\\
&{\|\langle v\rangle^{2d}\langle\nabla\rangle^{3}f_{0}\|_{L^{\infty}}}/{|\Re\omega\hbar k|}\penalty\ {\rm if}\penalty\ |\Re\omega|>4\hbar^{2}|k|^{2}.\end{split}\right.

Proof.

Step
1.
We begin by treating the lower bound of
\tilde{\varepsilon}(k,\omega)
:
We denote
\tilde{\omega}:=\frac{\Re\omega}{\hbar^{2}|k|^{2}}
,

(2.35)

\int\frac{f_{0}(v_{*})-f_{0}(v_{*}-\hbar k)}{\hbar k\cdot(v_{*}-\frac{1}{2}\hbar k)-\omega}\hskip 1.0pt\mathrm{d}{v_{*}}=\int\frac{f_{0}(v_{*}+\hbar k(\tfrac{1}{2}+\tilde{\omega})-f_{0}(v_{*}+\hbar k(-\tfrac{1}{2}+\tilde{\omega}))}{\hbar k\cdot v_{*}-i\Im\omega}\hskip 1.0pt\mathrm{d}{v_{*}}

Hence we can decompose it in real and imaginary parts

\displaystyle\Re\int\frac{f_{0}(v_{*})-f_{0}(v_{*}-\hbar k)}{\hbar k\cdot(v_{*}-\frac{1}{2}\hbar k)-\omega}\hskip 1.0pt\mathrm{d}{v_{*}}=

\displaystyle\int_{-\frac{1}{2}}^{\frac{1}{2}}\int\frac{(v_{*}\cdot\hbar k)\,\hbar k\cdot\nabla f\left(v_{*}+\hbar k\left(s+\tilde{\omega}\right)\right)}{(\hbar k\cdot v_{*})^{2}+(\Im\omega)^{2}}\hskip 1.0pt\mathrm{d}{v_{*}}\hskip 1.0pt\mathrm{d}{s}

\displaystyle\Im\int\frac{f_{0}(v_{*})-f_{0}(v_{*}-\hbar k)}{\hbar k\cdot(v_{*}-\frac{1}{2}\hbar k)-\omega}\hskip 1.0pt\mathrm{d}{v_{*}}=

\displaystyle\int_{-\frac{1}{2}}^{\frac{1}{2}}\hskip 1.0pt\mathrm{d}{s}\int\frac{\Im\omega\,\hbar k\cdot\nabla f\left(v_{*}+\hbar k\left(s+\tilde{\omega}\right)\right)}{(\hbar k\cdot v_{*})^{2}+(\Im\omega)^{2}}\hskip 1.0pt\mathrm{d}{v_{*}}

We only need to bound the real part of
\tilde{\varepsilon}
to get a bound from below. Using the orthogonal decomposition of
v_{*}
as
v_{*}:=v_{\shortparallel}+v_{\perp}
, where
v_{\perp}\cdot k=0
, and denoting
\tilde{v}_{s}:=\hbar\left(s+\tilde{\omega}\right)k
, we get for any constant
a>0

\begin{split}\bigg|\Re\int\frac{f_{0}(v_{*})-f_{0}(v_{*}-k)}{\hbar k\cdot(v_{*}-\frac{1}{2}\hbar k)-\omega}\hskip 1.0pt\mathrm{d}{v_{*}}\bigg|&\leq\int_{-\frac{1}{2}}^{\frac{1}{2}}\int_{|\hbar k\cdot v_{*}|\geq a}\frac{\hbar|k|\left|\nabla f\left(v_{*}+\tilde{v}_{s}\right)\right|}{a}\hskip 1.0pt\mathrm{d}{v_{*}}\hskip 1.0pt\mathrm{d}{s}\\
&+\int_{-\frac{1}{2}}^{\frac{1}{2}}\int_{|\hbar k\cdot v_{\shortparallel}|<a}\left|\frac{\hbar v_{\shortparallel}\cdot k\,\hbar k\cdot\left(\nabla f\left(v_{\shortparallel}+v_{\perp}+\tilde{v}_{s}\right)-\nabla f\left(-v_{\shortparallel}+v_{\perp}+\tilde{v}_{s}\right)\right)}{(\hbar k\cdot v_{\shortparallel})^{2}+(\Im\omega)^{2}}\right|\hskip 1.0pt\mathrm{d}{v}_{*}\hskip 1.0pt\mathrm{d}{s}\\
&\lesssim\left(\frac{\hbar|k|}{a}+\frac{a}{\hbar|k|}\right)\|\langle v\rangle^{2d}\langle\nabla\rangle^{2}f_{0}\|_{L^{\infty}}\end{split}

Choosing
a=\hbar|k|
, for some contant
c>0
,

|\tilde{\varepsilon}(k,\omega)|\geq 1-c\|\mathscr{V}\|_{L^{1}}\|\langle v\rangle^{2d}\langle\nabla\rangle^{2}f_{0}\|_{L^{\infty}}>0.

Step
2.
We treat know the estimation of
|1-\tilde{\varepsilon}(k,\omega)|
. The bound with respect to
|\Im\omega|^{-1}
can be deduced from our estimation of the lower bound of
|\tilde{\varepsilon}(k,\omega)|
.

For the bound with respect to
\Re\omega
, we suppose now that
\Re\omega>4\hbar^{2}|k|
. Then
\tilde{v}_{s}\cdot\hat{k}
is bigger than
\tfrac{\Re\omega}{4\hbar|k|}
and

\displaystyle\bigg|\int\frac{f_{0}(v_{*})-f_{0}(v_{*}-k)}{\hbar k\cdot(v_{*}-\frac{1}{2}\hbar k)-\omega}\hskip 1.0pt\mathrm{d}{v_{*}}\bigg|

\displaystyle\leq\int_{-\frac{1}{2}}^{\frac{1}{2}}\int_{|\hbar k\cdot v_{*}|\geq\frac{\Re\omega}{4}}\frac{\hbar|k|\left|\nabla f\left(v_{*}+\tilde{v}_{s}\right)\right|}{\Re\omega/4}\hskip 1.0pt\mathrm{d}{v_{*}}\hskip 1.0pt\mathrm{d}{s}

\displaystyle+\int_{-\frac{1}{2}}^{\frac{1}{2}}

\displaystyle\int_{|\hbar k\cdot v_{\shortparallel}|<\frac{\Re\omega}{4}}\left|\frac{\hbar v_{\shortparallel}\cdot k\,\hbar k\cdot\left(\nabla f\left(v_{\shortparallel}+v_{\perp}+\tilde{v}_{s}\right)-\nabla f\left(-v_{\shortparallel}+v_{\perp}+\tilde{v}_{s}\right)\right)}{(\hbar k\cdot v_{\shortparallel})^{2}+(\Im\omega)^{2}}\right|\hskip 1.0pt\mathrm{d}{v}_{*}\hskip 1.0pt\mathrm{d}{s}

\displaystyle+\int_{-\frac{1}{2}}^{\frac{1}{2}}

\displaystyle\int_{|\hbar k\cdot v_{\shortparallel}|<\frac{\Re\omega}{4}}\left|\frac{\Im\omega\,\hbar k\cdot\left(\nabla f\left(v_{\shortparallel}+v_{\perp}+\tilde{v}_{s}\right)+\nabla f\left(-v_{\shortparallel}+v_{\perp}+\tilde{v}_{s}\right)\right)}{(\hbar k\cdot v_{\shortparallel})^{2}+(\Im\omega)^{2}}\right|\hskip 1.0pt\mathrm{d}{v}_{*}\hskip 1.0pt\mathrm{d}{s}

\displaystyle\lesssim\left(\hbar|k|+1\right)\frac{\|\langle v\rangle^{2}\langle\nabla\rangle^{2}f_{0}\|_{L^{\infty}}}{\Re\omega},

where we use the estimation

\frac{1}{1+|v_{\shortparallel}+v_{\perp}+\tilde{v}_{s}|^{2d}}\lesssim\frac{1}{(1+|v_{\shortparallel}+\tilde{v}_{s}|^{d})(1+|v_{\perp}|^{d})}\lesssim\frac{\hbar|k|}{\Re\omega(1+|v_{\perp}|^{d})}

and the identity

\int_{\mathbb{R}}\frac{\hbar|k|\Im\omega\hskip 1.0pt\mathrm{d}{x}}{(\hbar|k|x)^{2}+(\Im\omega)^{2}}={\pi}.

We conclude by using the bound
{\hat{\mathscr{V}}}(k)=O(\langle k\rangle^{-1})
(by Assumption
1
).

Step
3.
Deriving (
2.35
) with respect to
\Re\omega
, one has

\displaystyle\tfrac{\partial}{\partial\Re\omega}\tilde{\varepsilon}(k,\omega)=\int\frac{k}{\hbar|k|^{2}}\cdot\frac{\nabla f_{0}(v_{*}+\hbar k(\tfrac{1}{2}+\tilde{\omega})-\nabla f_{0}(v_{*}+\hbar k(-\tfrac{1}{2}+\tilde{\omega}))}{\hbar k\cdot v_{*}-i\Im\omega}\hskip 1.0pt\mathrm{d}{v_{*}}.

The expected bound is obtained by using the same estimation than in Step 2.
∎

Fix
\beta\in\mathbb{R}
and
\omega\in\mathbb{C}\setminus\mathbb{R}
. We introduce now the function

\displaystyle\mathscr{B}(v_{1},k,\beta):=\left<\left(L_{2}+\beta-\frac{\omega}{2}\right)^{-1}\left(L_{1}-\beta-\frac{\omega}{2}\right)^{-1}A(v_{1},v_{2},k)\right>_{v_{2}}.

We want to compute the limit as
\omega\to 0
with
\Im\omega>|\Re\omega|
of
\frac{1}{2\pi i}\int_{\mathbb{R}}B(v_{1},k)\hskip 1.0pt\mathrm{d}\beta
. For
v_{3}\in\mathbb{{R}}^{d}
, we denote
v_{3}^{\prime}:=v_{3}+\hbar k
, and we apply the decomposition

(2.36)

\mathscr{B}(v_{1},k,\beta)=\frac{{\hat{\mathscr{V}}}(k)}{\tilde{\varepsilon}\left(k,\frac{\omega}{2}-\beta\right)}\left<\frac{f_{0}(v_{1})f_{0}(v_{2})-f_{0}(v_{1}^{\prime})f_{0}(v_{2}^{\prime})}{\left(\Delta_{-k}E(v_{1})-\beta-\frac{\omega}{2}\right)\left(\Delta_{k}E(v_{2})+\beta-\frac{\omega}{2}\right)}\right>_{\!\!\!v_{2}}\\
+\frac{-{\hat{\mathscr{V}}}(k)^{2}}{\tilde{\varepsilon}\left(k,\frac{\omega}{2}-\beta\right)\tilde{\varepsilon}\left(-k,\frac{\omega}{2}+\beta\right)}\frac{\Delta_{-k}f_{0}(v_{1})}{\Delta_{-k}E(v_{1})-\beta-\frac{\omega}{2}}\left<\frac{f_{0}(v_{3})f_{0}(v_{2})-f_{0}(v_{3}^{\prime})f_{0}(v_{2}^{\prime})}{\left(\Delta_{-k}E(k_{3})-\beta-\frac{\omega}{2}\right)\left(\Delta_{k}E(v_{2})+\beta-\frac{\omega}{2}\right)}\right>_{\!\!v_{2},v_{3}}

Using the identities

(2.37)

\displaystyle f_{0}(v_{1})f_{0}(v_{2})-f_{0}(v^{\prime}_{1})f_{0}(v^{\prime}_{2})=f_{0}(v_{1})\Delta_{k}f_{0}(v_{2})+\Delta_{-k}f_{0}(v_{1})f_{0}(v_{2}^{\prime}),

(2.38)

\displaystyle{\hat{\mathscr{V}}}(k)\left\langle\frac{\Delta_{k}f_{0}(v_{2})}{\Delta_{k}E(v_{2})+\beta-\frac{\omega}{2}}\right\rangle_{\!\!v_{2}}=\tilde{\varepsilon}(k,\tfrac{\omega}{2}-\beta)-1,

the first line of (
2.36
) becomes

\frac{\tilde{\varepsilon}\left(k,\frac{\omega}{2}-\beta\right)-1}{\tilde{\varepsilon}\left(k,\frac{\omega}{2}-\beta\right)}\frac{f_{0}(v_{1})}{\Delta_{-k}E(v_{1})-\beta-\frac{\omega}{2}}+\frac{{\hat{\mathscr{V}}}(k)}{\tilde{\varepsilon}\left(k,\frac{\omega}{2}-\beta\right)}\frac{\Delta_{-k}f_{0}(v_{1})}{\Delta_{-k}E(v_{1})-\beta-\frac{\omega}{2}}\left<\frac{f_{0}(v^{\prime}_{2})}{\Delta_{k}E(v_{2})+\beta-\frac{\omega}{2}}\right>_{\!\!v_{2}},

and the second line is decomposed as

\frac{{\hat{\mathscr{V}}}(k)\Delta_{-k}f_{0}(v_{1})}{\Delta_{-k}E(v_{1})-\beta-\frac{\omega}{2}}\bigg[\frac{1-\tilde{\varepsilon}\left(k,\frac{\omega}{2}-\beta\right)}{\tilde{\varepsilon}\left(k,\frac{\omega}{2}-\beta\right)\tilde{\varepsilon}\left(-k,\frac{\omega}{2}+\beta\right)}\left<\frac{f_{0}(v_{3})}{\Delta_{-k}E(v_{3})-\beta-\frac{\omega}{2}}\right>_{\!\!v_{3}}\\
+\frac{1-\tilde{\varepsilon}\left(-k,\frac{\omega}{2}+\beta\right)}{\tilde{\varepsilon}\left(k,\frac{\omega}{2}-\beta\right)\tilde{\varepsilon}\left(-k,\frac{\omega}{2}+\beta\right)}\left<\frac{f_{0}(v_{2}^{\prime})}{\Delta_{k}E(v_{2})+\beta-\frac{\omega}{2}}\right>_{\!\!v_{2}}\bigg].

Hence,
\mathscr{B}(v_{1},k,\beta)
can be decompose into 3 peaces
